<a id='question-016'></a>

### Question 016: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 16 (supply)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the vascular supply of the cerebral cortex, specifically the territories supplied by the anterior cerebral artery (ACA) and middle cerebral artery (MCA), and relating these territories to specific cortical areas and their functions. (理解大脑皮层的血管供应，特别是前大脑动脉 (ACA) 和中大脑动脉 (MCA) 供应的区域，以及这些区域与特定皮层区域及其功能的对应关系。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with acute onset of right-sided weakness and sensory loss. The weakness predominantly affects the right leg and foot, with less pronounced involvement of the right arm and face. Sensory examination reveals decreased sensation to light touch and pinprick on the right side of the body, again more pronounced in the leg and foot than the arm and face. The patient is alert and oriented, with no aphasia or visual field deficits. Cranial nerve examination is normal. MRI of the brain reveals an acute ischemic infarct in the left cerebral hemisphere. The infarct primarily involves the medial aspect of the left frontal and parietal lobes, sparing the lateral surface.

Which artery is most likely occluded, leading to this specific pattern of neurological deficits?

(A) Left anterior choroidal artery
(B) Left posterior cerebral artery
(C) Left middle cerebral artery (M1 segment)
(D) Left anterior cerebral artery (ACA)
(E) Left internal carotid artery

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 68-year-old male, history of vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia), acute onset right-sided weakness and sensory loss. This points towards an acute stroke affecting the motor and sensory pathways controlling the right side of the body.
- **Pattern of Deficits:** Weakness and sensory loss are *predominantly* in the right leg and foot, with *less* involvement of the right arm and face. This specific pattern is crucial. The leg/foot area is disproportionately affected compared to the arm/face.
- **Neurological Examination:** No aphasia (language function) or visual field deficits. This helps localize the lesion away from Broca's area, Wernicke's area, and the optic radiations/visual cortex. Cranial nerves are intact.
- **MRI Findings:** Acute ischemic infarct in the *left* cerebral hemisphere, involving the *medial* aspect of the left frontal and parietal lobes, sparing the lateral surface. This confirms a left hemisphere stroke and specifies the location.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the artery most likely occluded.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Cerebral Cortex Vascular Supply:** The cerebral cortex is primarily supplied by the anterior cerebral artery (ACA), middle cerebral artery (MCA), and posterior cerebral artery (PCA).
- **ACA Territory:** Supplies the medial aspect of the frontal and parietal lobes, including the motor and sensory cortex for the contralateral leg and foot (represented disproportionately large on the cortical homunculus). Also supplies the corpus callosum, anterior limb of the internal capsule, and parts of the basal ganglia. Occlusion typically causes contralateral leg/foot weakness and sensory loss > arm/face weakness and sensory loss. May cause behavioral changes, urinary incontinence, and grasp reflex.
- **MCA Territory:** Supplies the lateral aspect of the frontal, parietal, and temporal lobes, including the motor and sensory cortex for the contralateral face, arm, and trunk. Also supplies Broca's area (dominant hemisphere), Wernicke's area (dominant hemisphere), primary visual cortex (occipital lobe), and parts of the basal ganglia and internal capsule. Occlusion typically causes contralateral face/arm weakness and sensory loss > leg/foot weakness and sensory loss, aphasia (dominant hemisphere), neglect (non-dominant hemisphere), and visual field deficits (hemianopia).
- **PCA Territory:** Supplies the occipital lobe (visual cortex), inferior temporal lobe, thalamus, midbrain, and cerebellum. Occlusion typically causes contralateral homonymous hemianopia, memory impairment, and sometimes contralateral hemiparesis (via thalamic involvement).
- **Anterior Choroidal Artery:** A branch of the internal carotid artery, supplies parts of the internal capsule, basal ganglia, hippocampus, and choroid plexus. Occlusion can cause contralateral hemiparesis, hemisensory loss, and visual field deficits.
- **Internal Carotid Artery:** Supplies the ACA and MCA. Occlusion typically causes a massive stroke involving both ACA and MCA territories, leading to severe contralateral hemiplegia, hemisensory loss, aphasia (dominant), neglect (non-dominant), and visual field deficits.
- **Correlation:** The patient's symptoms (right leg/foot > right arm/face weakness and sensory loss) and MRI findings (medial left frontal/parietal infarct) perfectly match the territory supplied by the left ACA. The sparing of the lateral surface is also consistent with ACA involvement.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Left anterior choroidal artery:** Occlusion would affect the internal capsule and basal ganglia, causing contralateral hemiparesis and sensory loss, but typically not the specific leg/foot predominance seen here, nor the medial cortical infarct pattern. The infarct location described is primarily cortical, not deep structures like the internal capsule.
- (B) **Left posterior cerebral artery:** Occlusion would primarily affect the occipital lobe (visual cortex) and inferior temporal lobe, causing contralateral homonymous hemianopia and potentially memory issues. It does not supply the motor/sensory cortex for the leg/foot.
- (C) **Left middle cerebral artery (M1 segment):** Occlusion would affect the lateral surface of the hemisphere, causing contralateral face/arm > leg/foot weakness and sensory loss. This contradicts the patient's clinical presentation and MRI findings, which show medial involvement and leg/foot predominance.
- (E) **Left internal carotid artery:** Occlusion would cause a much larger infarct involving both ACA and MCA territories, leading to severe deficits affecting the face, arm, and leg, often with aphasia or neglect. The described infarct is localized to the medial aspect, sparing the lateral surface, which is inconsistent with a complete ICA occlusion.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure/Artery        | Territory Supplied                                  | Typical Deficits                                                                 | MRI Findings                                      |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ |
| **ACA (Correct)**       | Medial Frontal/Parietal Lobe (Leg/Foot Cortex)     | Contralateral Leg/Foot > Arm/Face Weakness/Sensory Loss, Behavioral Changes      | Medial Frontal/Parietal Infarct                   |
| MCA                     | Lateral Frontal/Parietal/Temporal Lobe (Face/Arm Cortex) | Contralateral Face/Arm > Leg/Foot Weakness/Sensory Loss, Aphasia, Neglect, Hemianopia | Lateral Frontal/Parietal/Temporal Infarct        |
| PCA                     | Occipital Lobe, Inferior Temporal Lobe             | Contralateral Homonymous Hemianopia, Memory Impairment                          | Occipital/Inferior Temporal Infarct               |
| Anterior Choroidal Artery | Internal Capsule, Basal Ganglia, Hippocampus       | Contralateral Hemiparesis/Sensory Loss, Visual Field Deficits                  | Deep Structure Infarct (Internal Capsule, etc.) |
| Internal Carotid Artery  | ACA & MCA Territories                             | Massive Stroke: Severe Hemiplegia/Hemisensory Loss, Aphasia/Neglect, Hemianopia | Large Infarct involving Medial & Lateral Cortex   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The specific pattern of motor and sensory deficits (leg/foot > arm/face) strongly suggests involvement of the anterior cerebral artery (ACA) territory, which supplies the medial aspect of the cerebral hemisphere, including the cortical representation for the lower limb. Differentiating ACA from MCA strokes is crucial based on the distribution of weakness and sensory loss. Remember that the cortical homunculus has a disproportionately large representation for the leg/foot in the medial frontal/parietal lobe (ACA territory) and the face/arm in the lateral lobe (MCA territory). Key differentials include MCA stroke (face/arm > leg/foot), PCA stroke (visual deficits), and internal carotid artery stroke (massive deficits involving both ACA and MCA territories).

<a id='question-017'></a>

### Question 017: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 17 (GENERAL)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the functional localization within the cerebral cortex, specifically the relationship between the primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus) and the corresponding body parts, and the vascular supply of this region (中动脉支配的皮层功能定位及血管分布).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with sudden onset of right-sided weakness and numbness, predominantly affecting his right arm and face. His speech is slightly slurred, but he can understand and respond appropriately. Neurological examination reveals right hemiparesis, more pronounced in the upper extremity (arm strength 3/5, hand strength 2/5) than the lower extremity (leg strength 4/5). Facial droop is noted on the right side. Sensory examination shows decreased sensation on the right side of the body, also more pronounced in the arm and face. Reflexes are hyperactive on the right side. An emergent non-contrast head CT scan shows a left middle cerebral artery (MCA) territory infarct.

Which area of the cerebral cortex is most likely affected by this infarct, leading to the observed clinical presentation?

(A) Postcentral gyrus
(B) Precentral gyrus
(C) Parietal lobe (excluding sensory cortex)
(D) Temporal lobe
(E) Occipital lobe

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia) presenting with acute onset right-sided weakness and numbness (hemiparesis and hemisensory loss).
- **Laterality:** Symptoms are on the right side of the body. This indicates a lesion in the contralateral (left) cerebral hemisphere.
- **Distribution:** Weakness and numbness affect the face and arm more than the leg. This suggests involvement of the cortical area representing these body parts.
- **Neurological Exam:** Right hemiparesis (weakness), right hemisensory loss, right facial droop, hyperreflexia on the right side. These are classic signs of an upper motor neuron lesion affecting the motor and sensory pathways.
- **Imaging:** CT scan shows a left MCA territory infarct. The MCA supplies a large portion of the lateral cerebral hemisphere, including the motor and sensory cortex for the contralateral face, arm, and trunk, as well as parts of the temporal and parietal lobes.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the specific area of the cerebral cortex affected.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Motor Cortex:** The primary motor cortex, responsible for voluntary movement, is located in the precentral gyrus of the frontal lobe. It is organized somatotopically (motor homunculus), with the face and upper limb represented in the lateral portion, and the lower limb represented medially. Damage to the precentral gyrus results in contralateral weakness (hemiparesis).
- **Sensory Cortex:** The primary somatosensory cortex, responsible for processing touch, temperature, pain, and proprioception, is located in the postcentral gyrus of the parietal lobe, immediately posterior to the central sulcus. Like the motor cortex, it is also somatotopically organized. Damage to the postcentral gyrus results in contralateral sensory loss (hemianesthesia).
- **MCA Territory:** The left MCA supplies the lateral surface of the left hemisphere, including the precentral gyrus (motor cortex for the right face, arm, trunk), postcentral gyrus (sensory cortex for the right face, arm, trunk), parts of the parietal and temporal lobes, and the basal ganglia and internal capsule.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's symptoms (right hemiparesis and hemisensory loss, more pronounced in the face and arm) are consistent with damage to the left precentral and postcentral gyri, which are supplied by the left MCA. The question asks for the area responsible for the *motor* deficit, which is the precentral gyrus. While the postcentral gyrus is also affected (explaining the sensory loss), the precentral gyrus is the primary motor area. The motor homunculus places the face and arm laterally, corresponding to the more severe symptoms in this patient.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Postcentral gyrus:** This is the primary somatosensory cortex. While it is also located in the MCA territory and would be affected by this infarct, it is primarily responsible for sensory function, not motor function. The patient has significant motor weakness (hemiparesis), making the precentral gyrus the more direct answer related to the motor deficit. The sensory loss is also present, but the question focuses on the cortex related to the *primary* motor function deficit.
- (C) **Parietal lobe (excluding sensory cortex):** The parietal lobe contains the somatosensory cortex (postcentral gyrus) but also areas involved in spatial processing, integration of sensory information, and language (in the dominant hemisphere). While parts of the parietal lobe are affected by an MCA infarct, the specific area responsible for the primary motor deficit is the precentral gyrus, which is part of the frontal lobe, not the parietal lobe.
- (D) **Temporal lobe:** The temporal lobe is primarily involved in auditory processing, memory, and language comprehension (Wernicke's area). While the MCA supplies parts of the temporal lobe, damage here typically causes auditory deficits, memory problems, or receptive aphasia, not the contralateral motor and sensory deficits described.
- (E) **Occipital lobe:** The occipital lobe is primarily responsible for visual processing. It is supplied mainly by the posterior cerebral artery (PCA). Damage here causes visual field defects (hemianopia), not motor or sensory deficits of the limbs and face.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure           | Location          | Primary Function                                  | Vascular Supply (Typical) | Clinical Deficit (Lesion)                                  |
| :------------------ | :---------------- | :----------------------------------------------- | :------------------------ | :---------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Precentral Gyrus** | Frontal Lobe      | Voluntary Motor Control                           | Anterior/Middle Cerebral Artery | Contralateral Hemiparesis/Hemiplegia                         |
| Postcentral Gyrus   | Parietal Lobe     | Somatosensory Processing (Touch, Pain, Temp, Proprioception) | Middle Cerebral Artery     | Contralateral Hemianesthesia/Sensory Loss                   |
| Parietal Lobe       | Between Frontal/Occipital | Sensory Integration, Spatial Awareness, Language (Dominant) | Middle Cerebral Artery     | Sensory deficits, spatial neglect, apraxia, agnosia, aphasia |
| Temporal Lobe       | Inferior Lateral  | Auditory Processing, Memory, Language Comprehension | Middle Cerebral Artery     | Auditory deficits, memory loss, receptive aphasia            |
| Occipital Lobe      | Posterior         | Visual Processing                                 | Posterior Cerebral Artery  | Visual Field Defects (Hemianopia)                            |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The primary motor cortex is located in the precentral gyrus of the frontal lobe, organized somatotopically. An MCA stroke affecting the left hemisphere typically causes right-sided motor and sensory deficits, predominantly affecting the face and arm. Differentiating the precentral gyrus (motor) from the postcentral gyrus (sensory) based on clinical deficits is crucial. Remember the motor homunculus (face/arm lateral, leg medial) and the MCA territory.

<a id='question-018'></a>

### Question 018: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 18 (FEATURES)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the functional localization within the cerebral cortex, specifically the primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus) and its relationship to the central sulcus, lateral sulcus, and underlying white matter tracts, as well as the vascular supply (middle cerebral artery). (理解大脑皮层的功能定位，特别是初级运动皮层（中央前回）与中央沟、侧脑裂以及潜在白质束的关系，以及其血管供应（大脑中动脉）。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with acute onset of right-sided hemiparesis and expressive aphasia. The symptoms began approximately 2 hours prior to arrival. Neurological examination reveals significant weakness in the right face, arm, and leg (strength 2/5), with sparing of the right eye muscles. Sensory examination is intact. Reflexes are hyperactive on the right side. Cranial nerve examination shows intact facial sensation and hearing. A non-contrast head CT scan reveals a left middle cerebral artery (MCA) territory infarct. Given the patient's symptoms and imaging findings, which of the following anatomical structures is most likely affected?

(A) Left superior temporal gyrus
(B) Left inferior frontal gyrus
(C) Left precentral gyrus
(D) Left postcentral gyrus
(E) Left supramarginal gyrus

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia). This increases the likelihood of a stroke.
- **Symptoms:** Acute onset right-sided hemiparesis (face, arm, leg) and expressive aphasia. The right-sided weakness indicates a lesion in the left cerebral hemisphere. Expressive aphasia (Broca's aphasia) points towards involvement of language areas, typically in the dominant (left) hemisphere.
- **Neurological Exam:** Right hemiparesis (face, arm, leg) confirms motor pathway involvement. Sparing of eye muscles suggests the lesion is distal to the corticobulbar nucleus for the oculomotor nerve (CN III) or involves the cortex controlling the face and limbs primarily. Hyperreflexia on the right side is consistent with an upper motor neuron lesion. Intact sensation rules out significant involvement of the primary somatosensory cortex (postcentral gyrus) or thalamus.
- **Imaging:** Left MCA territory infarct. The MCA supplies a large portion of the lateral cerebral hemisphere, including the frontal, temporal, and parietal lobes.
- **Question:** Which anatomical structure is most likely affected? We need to correlate the symptoms (right hemiparesis, expressive aphasia) with the location of the infarct within the left MCA territory.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Right Hemiparesis:** Weakness on the right side of the body is caused by damage to the left motor cortex (primary motor cortex, located in the precentral gyrus) or the descending corticospinal tract. The precentral gyrus contains the primary motor cortex, organized somatotopically (motor homunculus). The lateral aspect controls the face and arm, and the medial aspect controls the leg. The patient has weakness in the face, arm, and leg, suggesting involvement of a significant portion of the primary motor cortex.
- **Expressive Aphasia (Broca's Aphasia):** Difficulty producing speech is typically associated with damage to Broca's area, located in the inferior frontal gyrus (pars opercularis and pars triangularis) of the dominant hemisphere (usually the left).
- **MCA Territory:** The MCA supplies the lateral surface of the frontal, parietal, and temporal lobes, including the precentral gyrus, postcentral gyrus, superior temporal gyrus, middle temporal gyrus, inferior frontal gyrus (including Broca's area), and supramarginal gyrus.
- **Correlation:** The combination of right hemiparesis (affecting face, arm, leg) and expressive aphasia strongly points to an infarct involving both the primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus) and Broca's area (inferior frontal gyrus) within the left MCA territory. The precentral gyrus is the primary site for motor control, explaining the hemiparesis. The inferior frontal gyrus contains Broca's area, explaining the expressive aphasia.
- **Precentral Gyrus:** Located immediately anterior to the central sulcus. It contains the primary motor cortex (Brodmann area 4). Damage here causes contralateral weakness and paralysis.
- **Inferior Frontal Gyrus:** Located inferior to the precentral gyrus and superior to the lateral sulcus. It contains Broca's area (Brodmann areas 44 and 45). Damage here causes expressive aphasia.
- **Comparing Options:**
    - (A) Superior Temporal Gyrus: Primarily involved in auditory processing (Wernicke's area in the posterior part is associated with receptive aphasia). Damage here typically causes auditory deficits or receptive aphasia, not the motor deficits or expressive aphasia seen here.
    - (B) Inferior Frontal Gyrus: Contains Broca's area, explaining the expressive aphasia. However, it does not directly explain the extensive right hemiparesis (face, arm, leg). While the inferior frontal gyrus is part of the MCA territory, the motor symptoms point more strongly to the precentral gyrus.
    - (C) Precentral Gyrus: Contains the primary motor cortex, explaining the right hemiparesis (face, arm, leg). It is located within the MCA territory. While it doesn't directly explain the expressive aphasia, a large MCA infarct often affects adjacent structures like the inferior frontal gyrus. Given the prominent motor symptoms, the precentral gyrus is a highly likely site of involvement.
    - (D) Postcentral Gyrus: Contains the primary somatosensory cortex. Damage here causes contralateral sensory loss, which is absent in this patient.
    - (E) Supramarginal Gyrus: Part of the parietal lobe, involved in language processing (phonological processing) and spatial cognition. Damage can cause conduction aphasia or Gerstmann syndrome, not the primary symptoms presented.

- **Conclusion:** The most prominent feature is the right hemiparesis affecting the face, arm, and leg, which is directly attributable to damage in the left primary motor cortex located in the precentral gyrus. While expressive aphasia suggests involvement of the inferior frontal gyrus, the motor symptoms are the defining feature pointing to the precentral gyrus. A large MCA infarct could easily affect both areas. Therefore, the precentral gyrus is the most likely structure primarily responsible for the most significant clinical findings (hemiparesis).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Left superior temporal gyrus: Incorrect. This area is primarily involved in auditory processing and contains Wernicke's area (posterior part), associated with receptive aphasia. Damage here would not cause the motor deficits (hemiparesis) seen in the patient.
- (B) Left inferior frontal gyrus: Incorrect. This area contains Broca's area and is responsible for expressive aphasia, which the patient has. However, it is not the primary motor cortex and does not directly explain the extensive right hemiparesis (face, arm, leg). While likely involved in the infarct, the precentral gyrus better explains the motor findings.
- (D) Left postcentral gyrus: Incorrect. This area contains the primary somatosensory cortex. Damage here would cause contralateral sensory loss, which is explicitly stated as absent in the patient's examination.
- (E) Left supramarginal gyrus: Incorrect. This area is part of the parietal lobe and is involved in language processing (phonological loop) and spatial awareness. Damage can cause conduction aphasia or elements of Gerstmann syndrome, neither of which matches the patient's presentation of expressive aphasia and hemiparesis.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                 | Location                               | Function                                       | Symptoms of Lesion                                  | Vascular Supply (Primary) | USMLE Relevance                                                                 |
| :------------------------ | :------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------ | :----------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Precentral Gyrus**      | Anterior to Central Sulcus             | Primary Motor Cortex (Brodmann Area 4)         | Contralateral weakness/paralysis (face, arm, leg) | Middle Cerebral Artery   | **Correct Answer**. Key for motor function, motor homunculus.                   |
| Postcentral Gyrus         | Posterior to Central Sulcus            | Primary Somatosensory Cortex (Brodmann Areas 3, 1, 2) | Contralateral sensory loss                          | Middle Cerebral Artery   | Often confused with precentral gyrus; key for sensory function.                |
| Inferior Frontal Gyrus    | Inferior to Precentral Gyrus           | Broca's Area (Brodmann Areas 44, 45)           | Expressive Aphasia (Broca's)                        | Middle Cerebral Artery   | Key for language production. Often involved with precentral gyrus in MCA stroke. |
| Superior Temporal Gyrus   | Superior to Lateral Sulcus             | Auditory Processing, Wernicke's Area (posterior) | Auditory deficits, Receptive Aphasia (Wernicke's)   | Middle Cerebral Artery   | Key for auditory processing and language comprehension.                         |
| Supramarginal Gyrus       | Parietal Lobe, superior to Sylvian fissure | Language processing, spatial cognition          | Conduction Aphasia, Gerstmann Syndrome             | Middle Cerebral Artery   | Part of parietal lobe, involved in complex language functions.                 |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The primary motor cortex is located in the precentral gyrus, immediately anterior to the central sulcus. Lesions here cause contralateral weakness or paralysis, often affecting the face and arm more than the leg due to the somatotopic organization (motor homunculus). The inferior frontal gyrus contains Broca's area and is associated with expressive aphasia. Both structures are commonly affected by MCA strokes. Differentiating between motor cortex (precentral gyrus) and sensory cortex (postcentral gyrus) lesions based on motor vs. sensory deficits is crucial. Understanding the functional localization within the cerebral cortex is essential for localizing neurological deficits.

### Question 019: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 19 (surface)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the functional localization within the cerebral cortex, specifically the relationship between the primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus) and the internal capsule, and how lesions in these areas manifest clinically. (理解大脑皮层的功能定位，特别是初级运动皮层（中央前回）与内囊之间的关系，以及这些区域病变如何表现出临床症状。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with sudden onset of right-sided weakness and numbness affecting his face, arm, and leg. The symptoms began approximately 2 hours ago while he was watching television. On examination, his vital signs are stable. Neurological examination reveals right hemiparesis (strength 3/5 in the right upper and lower extremities) and right hemisensory loss (decreased sensation to light touch and pinprick). Facial sensation and motor function on the right side are also impaired. Reflexes are hyperactive on the right side, and a positive Babinski sign is present on the right. MRI of the brain reveals an acute ischemic infarct in the left hemisphere. The lesion is located deep within the white matter, medial to the caudate nucleus and lateral to the internal capsule, extending superiorly towards the precentral gyrus.

Which of the following structures is MOST likely affected by this infarct, leading to the patient's clinical presentation?

(A) Superior Temporal Gyrus
(B) Middle Frontal Gyrus
(C) Precentral Gyrus
(D) Postcentral Gyrus
(E) Angular Gyrus

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male, sudden onset right hemiparesis and hemisensory loss (face, arm, leg). This points to a contralateral (left hemisphere) lesion affecting motor and sensory pathways.
- **Neurological Exam:** Right hemiparesis (weakness), right hemisensory loss, right facial weakness/sensory loss. Hyperreflexia and positive Babinski sign on the right indicate an upper motor neuron lesion.
- **Imaging:** Acute ischemic infarct in the left hemisphere, deep white matter, medial to caudate nucleus, lateral to internal capsule, extending superiorly towards the precentral gyrus. This location is critical. The internal capsule contains major ascending (sensory) and descending (motor) tracts. The precentral gyrus is the primary motor cortex. The infarct's location near the internal capsule and extending towards the precentral gyrus strongly implicates these structures.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely affected structure causing the symptoms.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Precentral Gyrus (中央前回):** Located on the posterior aspect of the frontal lobe, immediately anterior to the central sulcus. It constitutes the primary motor cortex (Brodmann area 4). It contains large pyramidal neurons (Betz cells) whose axons descend in the corticospinal tract, controlling voluntary movement. Damage here causes contralateral weakness (hemiparesis/hemiplegia), often affecting the face, arm, and leg in a somatotopic manner (motor homunculus).
- **Internal Capsule (内囊):** A large white matter structure situated between the thalamus and the basal ganglia (caudate nucleus and putamen). It contains ascending sensory fibers (thalamocortical radiations) and descending motor fibers (corticospinal, corticobulbar tracts). Lesions here cause contralateral sensorimotor deficits (hemiparesis, hemisensory loss, often affecting the face, arm, and leg).
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's symptoms (right hemiparesis, right hemisensory loss, right facial involvement) are classic signs of a lesion affecting the left motor and sensory pathways. The MRI findings place the infarct deep within the white matter, near the internal capsule and extending towards the precentral gyrus. A lesion in this location would affect both the descending corticospinal/corticobulbar tracts within the internal capsule (causing motor deficits) and potentially the adjacent precentral gyrus itself (primary motor cortex) or its efferent fibers. Given the extension towards the precentral gyrus and the classic motor/sensory deficits, the precentral gyrus is a highly likely structure involved, either directly or through involvement of its outflow tracts. The internal capsule is also involved, but the question asks for the cortical structure most likely affected, and the infarct extends *towards* the precentral gyrus. The precentral gyrus is the origin of the motor commands that are disrupted.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Superior Temporal Gyrus (上颞小叶):** Located in the temporal lobe, involved in auditory processing (primary auditory cortex) and language comprehension (Wernicke's area, usually in the dominant hemisphere). Lesions typically cause auditory deficits or receptive aphasia, not contralateral motor/sensory deficits.
- (B) **Middle Frontal Gyrus (中额回):** Located in the frontal lobe, anterior to the precentral gyrus. It is part of the prefrontal cortex, involved in executive functions, working memory, and motor planning. Lesions can cause personality changes, impaired judgment, or subtle motor deficits, but not the classic contralateral hemiparesis/hemisensory loss seen here.
- (D) **Postcentral Gyrus (顶后小叶):** Located on the anterior aspect of the parietal lobe, immediately posterior to the central sulcus. It constitutes the primary somatosensory cortex (Brodmann areas 3, 1, 2). It receives sensory information from the thalamus. Damage here causes contralateral sensory loss (hemianesthesia), often affecting the face, arm, and leg in a somatotopic manner. While the patient has sensory loss, the primary motor deficits (hemiparesis) point more strongly towards involvement of the motor cortex (precentral gyrus) or its descending pathways (internal capsule). The infarct is described as extending *towards* the precentral gyrus, making it a likely site of involvement.
- (E) **Angular Gyrus (角回):** Located in the parietal lobe, superior to the temporal lobe. It is involved in language processing (reading, writing), number processing, and spatial cognition. Lesions can cause Gerstmann syndrome (agraphia, acalculia, finger agnosia, left-right disorientation) or alexia without agraphia, not contralateral motor/sensory deficits.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure             | Location                                  | Function                                      | Lesion Manifestations                                  | Relation to Vignette                                  |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|
| **Precentral Gyrus** | Frontal lobe, anterior to central sulcus | Primary Motor Cortex (voluntary movement)      | Contralateral hemiparesis/hemiplegia, motor deficits | Infarct extends *towards* it; explains motor deficits |
| Postcentral Gyrus    | Parietal lobe, posterior to central sulcus | Primary Somatosensory Cortex (sensory input)   | Contralateral hemisensory loss                       | Explains sensory deficits, but less likely primary site |
| Internal Capsule     | Deep white matter, medial to caudate      | Contains motor & sensory tracts                | Contralateral hemiparesis/hemisensory loss            | Infarct located near it; explains both deficits       |
| Superior Temporal Gyrus| Temporal lobe, superior surface           | Auditory processing, language comprehension   | Auditory deficits, receptive aphasia                 | Incorrect location, wrong function                   |
| Middle Frontal Gyrus | Frontal lobe, anterior to precentral gyrus| Executive function, motor planning             | Personality changes, subtle motor deficits           | Incorrect location, wrong function                   |
| Angular Gyrus        | Parietal lobe, near occipital-temporal junction| Language, number processing, spatial cognition | Gerstmann syndrome, alexia without agraphia          | Incorrect location, wrong function                   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The precentral gyrus (primary motor cortex) and the postcentral gyrus (primary somatosensory cortex) are located immediately anterior and posterior to the central sulcus, respectively. Lesions affecting the precentral gyrus cause contralateral motor deficits (hemiparesis), while lesions affecting the postcentral gyrus cause contralateral sensory deficits (hemianesthesia). The internal capsule, located deep to these gyri, contains major ascending and descending tracts, and lesions here often cause combined sensorimotor deficits. Differentiating lesions based on location (cortex vs. white matter tracts) and the specific deficits (motor vs. sensory vs. combined) is crucial. Key differentials include lesions in the postcentral gyrus (sensory only), internal capsule (combined sensorimotor), and other frontal/parietal lobe areas involved in different functions.

<a id='question-020'></a>

### Question 20: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 20 (cerebral)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the functional localization within the cerebral cortex, specifically the relationship between the primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus) and the internal capsule, and the consequences of vascular lesions affecting this region. (理解大脑皮层的功能定位，特别是初级运动皮层（中央前回）与内囊之间的关系，以及血管性病变对该区域的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with acute onset right-sided weakness and numbness. The symptoms began approximately 2 hours prior to arrival. On examination, his vital signs are stable. Neurological examination reveals significant weakness in the right upper and lower extremities (strength 2/5), particularly affecting the face and arm more than the leg. Sensory examination shows decreased sensation to light touch and pinprick on the right side of the body, also more pronounced in the face and arm. Reflexes are brisk on the right side, and a positive Babinski sign is present on the right. Cranial nerve examination is intact, including facial muscle strength and sensation. Ocular movements are full, and visual fields are normal. An emergent non-contrast head CT scan shows a small area of hypodensity in the left internal capsule.

Which of the following structures is most likely affected by the lesion described?

(A) Broca's area
(B) Wernicke's area
(C) Primary somatosensory cortex
(D) Primary motor cortex
(E) Visual cortex

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia) suggests a potential stroke.
- **Onset:** Acute onset (2 hours) points towards a vascular event (ischemic or hemorrhagic stroke).
- **Symptoms:** Right-sided weakness (hemiparesis) and numbness (hemianesthesia) indicate a lesion affecting the contralateral (left) side of the brain.
- **Distribution:** Weakness and sensory loss affect the face, arm, and leg, but are more pronounced in the face and arm. This pattern is characteristic of involvement of the upper motor neuron pathways controlling these regions.
- **Neurological Exam:** Right hemiparesis (2/5 strength), right hemianesthesia, brisk reflexes, and positive Babinski sign on the right confirm an upper motor neuron lesion affecting the corticospinal and spinothalamic tracts.
- **Cranial Nerves:** Intact cranial nerves (especially facial motor and sensory) and ocular movements rule out brainstem or specific cranial nerve involvement.
- **Imaging:** Non-contrast head CT shows a hypodensity in the left internal capsule. This is a key finding localizing the lesion.
- **Question:** Asks for the structure most likely affected by the lesion in the left internal capsule, given the clinical presentation.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Internal Capsule:** The internal capsule is a white matter structure situated between the thalamus and the basal ganglia (caudate nucleus and lentiform nucleus). It contains ascending sensory fibers (thalamocortical radiations) and descending motor fibers (corticospinal, corticobulbar, corticopontine tracts).
- **Location of Lesion:** The lesion is in the left internal capsule.
- **Corticospinal Tract:** This major descending motor pathway originates in the primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus, Brodmann area 4) and descends through the internal capsule, brainstem, and spinal cord. It controls voluntary movement of the contralateral limbs and trunk. Fibers for the face and arm are located more superiorly/anteriorly within the internal capsule compared to fibers for the leg.
- **Spinothalamic Tract:** This major ascending sensory pathway carries pain, temperature, and crude touch information from the spinal cord to the thalamus and then to the primary somatosensory cortex (postcentral gyrus, Brodmann areas 3, 1, 2).
- **Clinical Correlation:** A lesion in the left internal capsule affects both the descending corticospinal tract (causing right hemiparesis, more pronounced in the face and arm) and the ascending spinothalamic tract (causing right hemianesthesia). The upper motor neuron signs (brisk reflexes, Babinski) are consistent with corticospinal tract damage.
- **Primary Motor Cortex (Precentral Gyrus):** This is the origin of the corticospinal tract. Fibers from the primary motor cortex pass through the internal capsule on their way to the brainstem and spinal cord. Therefore, a lesion in the internal capsule affecting these fibers will produce motor deficits corresponding to the area of the cortex from which the fibers originate. The precentral gyrus is the most direct cortical structure related to the motor fibers passing through the internal capsule.
- **Other Options:**
    - (A) Broca's area (inferior frontal gyrus, usually left hemisphere): Involved in speech production. Lesions cause expressive aphasia. Not consistent with the presentation.
    - (B) Wernicke's area (superior temporal gyrus, usually left hemisphere): Involved in language comprehension. Lesions cause receptive aphasia. Not consistent with the presentation.
    - (C) Primary somatosensory cortex (postcentral gyrus): Receives sensory information. While sensory fibers pass through the internal capsule, the primary motor cortex is the origin of the motor fibers most prominently affected, explaining the significant motor deficit. The question asks for the structure *most likely affected* by the lesion *in the internal capsule* given the motor symptoms. The motor cortex is the source of the affected motor fibers.
    - (E) Visual cortex (occipital lobe): Processes visual information. Lesions cause visual field defects. Not consistent with the presentation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Broca's area: Located in the inferior frontal gyrus. Lesions typically cause expressive aphasia (difficulty producing speech). The patient has no speech deficits described. The internal capsule is posterior and inferior to Broca's area. Incorrect.
- (B) Wernicke's area: Located in the superior temporal gyrus. Lesions typically cause receptive aphasia (difficulty understanding speech). The patient has no language deficits described. The internal capsule is posterior and inferior to Wernicke's area. Incorrect.
- (C) Primary somatosensory cortex: Located in the postcentral gyrus, posterior to the precentral gyrus. While sensory fibers (spinothalamic tract) pass through the internal capsule and are affected in this case (causing hemianesthesia), the motor deficits (hemiparesis) are often more prominent in internal capsule strokes, and the primary motor cortex is the origin of the affected motor fibers. The question asks which structure is *most likely affected* by the lesion *in the internal capsule* given the motor symptoms. The motor cortex is the source of the corticospinal tract fibers. Incorrect.
- (E) Visual cortex: Located in the occipital lobe. Lesions cause visual field defects (hemianopia, quadrantanopia). The patient has no visual symptoms described. The internal capsule is far anterior to the visual cortex. Incorrect.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                     | Location                                  | Function                                     | Lesion Effects                                                                 | Relationship to Internal Capsule |
| :--------------------------- | :---------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------ |
| Primary Motor Cortex (D)      | Precentral gyrus, frontal lobe             | Voluntary motor control                      | Contralateral hemiparesis/hemiplegia (face/arm > leg)                           | Origin of motor fibers in capsule |
| Primary Somatosensory Cortex (C) | Postcentral gyrus, parietal lobe            | Sensory processing (touch, pain, temp)       | Contralateral hemianesthesia                                                    | Destination of sensory fibers in capsule |
| Broca's Area (A)             | Inferior frontal gyrus, frontal lobe       | Speech production                            | Expressive aphasia                                                              | Anterior to capsule             |
| Wernicke's Area (B)          | Superior temporal gyrus, temporal lobe     | Language comprehension                       | Receptive aphasia                                                               | Anterior to capsule             |
| Visual Cortex (E)             | Occipital lobe                             | Visual processing                            | Visual field defects (hemianopia)                                               | Posterior to capsule            |
| Internal Capsule             | Between thalamus & basal ganglia           | Contains motor (corticospinal) & sensory (spinothalamic) tracts | Contralateral hemiparesis/hemiplegia & hemianesthesia (face/arm > leg) | Contains tracts from/to cortex   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
A lesion in the internal capsule typically affects both motor and sensory pathways, leading to contralateral hemiparesis and hemianesthesia. The motor deficits often involve the face and arm more than the leg due to the somatotopic organization of the corticospinal tract within the capsule. The primary motor cortex is the origin of the corticospinal tract fibers passing through the internal capsule, making it the cortical area most directly related to the motor deficits caused by such a lesion. Remember the classic internal capsule stroke syndrome: contralateral face/arm/leg weakness and sensory loss. Key differentials include lacunar infarcts in other locations (e.g., pons, thalamus) or larger cortical strokes, but the internal capsule is a common site for lacunar infarcts causing this specific syndrome.

<a id='question-021'></a>

### Question 21: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 21 (cortex)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the functional localization within the cerebral cortex, specifically the primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus) and its relationship to the internal capsule, and the resulting motor deficits following a vascular event affecting this region. (理解大脑皮层的功能定位，特别是初级运动皮层（中央前回）与内囊的关系，以及血管事件影响该区域后产生的运动功能障碍。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with acute onset of right-sided weakness and difficulty speaking. He reports that the symptoms began suddenly 30 minutes prior while watching television. On examination, his vital signs are stable. Neurological examination reveals right hemiparesis, predominantly affecting the face and arm (strength 2/5), with relative sparing of the leg (strength 4/5). He exhibits dysarthria and mild right facial droop. Sensory examination is intact. Reflexes are hyperactive on the right side. A non-contrast head CT scan shows a small area of hypodensity in the left internal capsule.

Which of the following cortical areas is MOST likely affected by the lesion causing these symptoms?

(A) Primary Somatosensory Cortex (Postcentral Gyrus)
(B) Primary Visual Cortex (Occipital Lobe)
(C) Primary Motor Cortex (Precentral Gyrus)
(D) Broca's Area (Inferior Frontal Gyrus)
(E) Wernicke's Area (Superior Temporal Gyrus)

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia) suggests a potential stroke.
- **Onset:** Acute onset of focal neurological deficits points towards a vascular event (stroke).
- **Symptoms:** Right-sided weakness (hemiparesis) affecting face and arm more than the leg, dysarthria (difficulty speaking), and right facial droop. This pattern suggests involvement of the motor pathways controlling the right side of the body.
- **Sensory Examination:** Intact sensation rules out significant involvement of the primary somatosensory cortex or major sensory pathways.
- **Reflexes:** Hyperreflexia on the right side indicates an upper motor neuron lesion.
- **Imaging:** CT scan showing a lesion in the left internal capsule confirms a lesion affecting descending motor pathways.
- **Question:** Asks for the *cortical area* most likely affected, given the internal capsule lesion and resulting motor deficits.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Internal Capsule:** The internal capsule is a white matter structure located deep within the cerebral hemispheres, between the thalamus and the basal ganglia. It contains ascending sensory fibers (thalamocortical radiations) and descending motor fibers (corticospinal, corticobulbar tracts).
- **Motor Pathways:** The corticospinal tract originates primarily in the primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus, Brodmann area 4) in the frontal lobe. Fibers from the motor cortex descend through the internal capsule. The corticobulbar tract also originates in the motor cortex and descends through the internal capsule to innervate cranial nerve motor nuclei in the brainstem.
- **Lesion Location:** A lesion in the left internal capsule affects the descending motor fibers originating from the contralateral (right) motor cortex. These fibers control motor function on the *opposite* side of the body (left side). However, the question asks which *cortical area* is affected, implying the *origin* of the damaged fibers.
- **Motor Homunculus:** The primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus) is organized somatotopically, represented by the motor homunculus. The face and upper limb have disproportionately large representations compared to the lower limb. This explains why the patient has more severe weakness in the face and arm than the leg.
- **Clinical Correlation:** A lesion in the left internal capsule affecting the corticospinal and corticobulbar tracts originating from the right primary motor cortex would cause contralateral (left) hemiparesis, predominantly affecting the face and arm, along with dysarthria (due to corticobulbar tract involvement affecting muscles of speech) and facial droop (due to corticobulbar tract involvement affecting facial muscles). The patient's symptoms (right-sided weakness, face/arm > leg, dysarthria, facial droop) are consistent with a lesion affecting the motor pathways originating from the *right* primary motor cortex, which pass through the *left* internal capsule. The question asks for the cortical area *affected*, which refers to the origin of the damaged fibers, i.e., the right primary motor cortex.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Primary Somatosensory Cortex (Postcentral Gyrus):** Located posterior to the central sulcus, this area receives sensory information. A lesion here would cause contralateral sensory loss (hemianesthesia), not primarily motor weakness. The patient's sensory exam was intact.
- (B) **Primary Visual Cortex (Occipital Lobe):** Located in the occipital lobe, this area processes visual information. A lesion here would cause visual field defects (e.g., homonymous hemianopia), not motor weakness or speech difficulties.
- (D) **Broca's Area (Inferior Frontal Gyrus):** Located in the inferior frontal gyrus (usually left hemisphere), this area is crucial for speech production. A lesion here causes Broca's aphasia (expressive aphasia), characterized by difficulty forming words and sentences, but typically does not cause contralateral motor weakness unless it extends to involve the adjacent motor cortex or descending pathways. While the patient has dysarthria, the primary deficit is motor weakness, and the lesion is in the internal capsule, not primarily Broca's area.
- (E) **Wernicke's Area (Superior Temporal Gyrus):** Located in the superior temporal gyrus (usually left hemisphere), this area is crucial for language comprehension. A lesion here causes Wernicke's aphasia (receptive aphasia), characterized by difficulty understanding language, fluent but nonsensical speech, and unawareness of errors. It does not cause motor weakness.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature                 | Primary Motor Cortex (Precentral Gyrus) | Primary Somatosensory Cortex (Postcentral Gyrus) | Broca's Area (Inferior Frontal Gyrus) | Wernicke's Area (Superior Temporal Gyrus) | Primary Visual Cortex (Occipital Lobe) |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------ | :--------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------- |
| **Location**            | Frontal Lobe, anterior to central sulcus | Parietal Lobe, posterior to central sulcus      | Frontal Lobe, inferior frontal gyrus  | Temporal Lobe, superior temporal gyrus   | Occipital Lobe                         |
| **Primary Function**    | Voluntary Motor Control                  | Sensory Perception (Touch, Pain, Temp, Proprio) | Speech Production                     | Language Comprehension                  | Visual Processing                      |
| **Lesion Manifestation** | Contralateral Hemiparesis/Hemiplegia    | Contralateral Sensory Loss                     | Expressive Aphasia                    | Receptive Aphasia                       | Visual Field Defect                    |
| **Relationship to Internal Capsule** | Origin of corticospinal/bulbar tracts passing through | Termination of thalamocortical radiations passing through | Adjacent to motor cortex/pathways      | Not directly related                   | Not directly related                   |
| **Motor Homunculus**    | Yes (Face/Arm > Leg)                     | Yes (Face/Arm > Leg)                            | No                                    | No                                       | No                                     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus) controls voluntary movement, and its fibers descend through the internal capsule. Lesions in the internal capsule often affect these descending motor pathways, causing contralateral weakness. The motor homunculus explains the disproportionate weakness in the face and arm compared to the leg. Differentiating motor cortex lesions from sensory cortex, visual cortex, and language areas (Broca's/Wernicke's) is crucial based on the specific neurological deficits. Remember that lesions *within* the internal capsule affect fibers passing *through* it, originating from the cortex (motor) or terminating in the cortex (sensory).

<a id='question-022'></a>

### Question 22: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 22 (highly)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the functional localization within the cerebral cortex, specifically the precentral gyrus (primary motor cortex) and its relationship to the internal capsule, and the consequences of lesions affecting these areas. (理解大脑皮层的功能定位，特别是中央前回（初级运动皮层）与内囊的关系，以及损伤这些区域的后果。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with sudden onset right-sided weakness and numbness affecting his face, arm, and leg. The symptoms began approximately 2 hours ago while he was watching television. On examination, his vital signs are stable. Neurological examination reveals right hemiparesis (strength 3/5 in the right upper extremity and 4/5 in the right lower extremity), right hemisensory loss to light touch and pinprick, and a right homonymous hemianopia. Cranial nerve examination shows mild weakness of the right lower facial muscles (sparing the forehead). Reflexes are hyperactive on the right side. An emergent non-contrast head CT scan shows no evidence of hemorrhage. MRI of the brain reveals an acute ischemic infarct in the left middle cerebral artery (MCA) territory, specifically involving the lateral aspect of the left frontal and parietal lobes, including the precentral gyrus and extending into the underlying white matter.

Which of the following structures is MOST likely affected by the infarct, leading to the observed motor deficits?

(A) Internal capsule (posterior limb)
(B) Basal ganglia (putamen)
(C) Thalamus (ventral posterior nucleus)
(D) Cerebellum (left hemisphere)
(E) Pons (basis pontis)

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia). This increases the likelihood of a stroke.
- **Onset:** Sudden onset of right-sided neurological deficits suggests a vascular event (stroke).
- **Symptoms:** Right hemiparesis (face, arm, leg), right hemisensory loss (face, arm, leg), right homonymous hemianopia. This pattern points to a lesion in the left cerebral hemisphere affecting motor, sensory, and visual pathways.
- **Cranial Nerve Exam:** Weakness of the right lower facial muscles (sparing the forehead) indicates an upper motor neuron lesion affecting the facial nerve nucleus contralaterally (left hemisphere). The forehead receives bilateral cortical innervation, hence sparing.
- **Reflexes:** Right-sided hyperreflexia is consistent with an upper motor neuron lesion.
- **Imaging:** MRI confirms an acute ischemic infarct in the left MCA territory, involving the lateral frontal and parietal lobes, including the precentral gyrus and underlying white matter.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the structure MOST likely affected leading to the motor deficits. The motor deficits (right hemiparesis) are contralateral to the lesion (left hemisphere). The lesion involves the precentral gyrus (primary motor cortex) and underlying white matter. The motor pathways descend from the cortex through the internal capsule.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Precentral Gyrus:** Located in the frontal lobe, immediately anterior to the central sulcus. It constitutes the primary motor cortex (Brodmann area 4). Neurons in the precentral gyrus control voluntary movements of the contralateral body. The homunculus representation is inverted (legs medial, face lateral).
- **Motor Pathways:** Corticospinal tract neurons originate in the motor cortex (primarily precentral gyrus). Axons project downwards through the corona radiata (white matter radiating from the cortex) and converge in the internal capsule.
- **Internal Capsule:** A white matter structure situated between the thalamus and the basal ganglia (caudate nucleus and lentiform nucleus). It contains ascending sensory fibers (thalamocortical radiations) and descending motor fibers (corticospinal and corticobulbar tracts). The posterior limb of the internal capsule contains the corticospinal tract fibers responsible for motor control of the limbs and trunk. The genu contains corticobulbar fibers for the face.
- **MCA Stroke:** The MCA supplies blood to the lateral surface of the cerebral hemisphere, including the frontal, parietal, and temporal lobes. A stroke in the MCA territory often affects the precentral gyrus (motor cortex), postcentral gyrus (sensory cortex), and underlying white matter (corona radiata and internal capsule).
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's right hemiparesis is caused by damage to the left motor cortex (precentral gyrus) and/or the descending motor pathways (corticospinal tract) as they pass through the internal capsule. The MRI findings confirm involvement of the precentral gyrus and underlying white matter. Damage to the corticospinal tract fibers within the internal capsule (specifically the posterior limb, which carries leg and trunk fibers, and the genu, which carries face and arm fibers) would directly cause contralateral hemiparesis. Given the involvement of the precentral gyrus and underlying white matter extending towards the internal capsule, the internal capsule is a highly likely structure affected, mediating the motor deficits.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Internal capsule (posterior limb)**: This structure contains the corticospinal tract fibers descending from the motor cortex. A lesion here, often caused by MCA stroke affecting the corona radiata and internal capsule, directly causes contralateral hemiparesis, matching the patient's symptoms. The infarct involves the precentral gyrus and underlying white matter, which includes the corona radiata leading into the internal capsule. This is the most likely structure responsible for the motor deficits.
- (B): **Basal ganglia (putamen)**: The putamen is part of the lentiform nucleus. While lesions here can cause motor symptoms (e.g., Parkinsonism, chorea, dystonia, hemiballismus), they typically do not cause pure hemiparesis with sensory loss and homonymous hemianopia. The putamen is primarily involved in motor control, learning, and habit formation, but damage usually presents differently than this patient's presentation. The MCA stroke affects the cortex and white matter, not typically the deep basal ganglia structures primarily, although branches can supply parts of it.
- (C): **Thalamus (ventral posterior nucleus)**: The ventral posterior nucleus (VPL) of the thalamus relays sensory information (touch, pain, temperature, proprioception) from the body to the somatosensory cortex (postcentral gyrus). A lesion here would cause contralateral sensory loss, which the patient has. However, it does not directly explain the motor deficits (hemiparesis). While thalamic strokes can occur with MCA infarcts, the primary cause of the motor deficit in this case, given the cortical involvement, is the damage to the motor cortex and its descending pathways.
- (D): **Cerebellum (left hemisphere)**: The cerebellum is primarily involved in coordination, balance, and motor learning. Lesions typically cause ataxia, dysmetria, intention tremor, and nystagmus. While cerebellar strokes can occur, they do not cause contralateral hemiparesis and sensory loss. The patient's symptoms are characteristic of an upper motor neuron lesion affecting the cerebral cortex and its descending pathways.
- (E): **Pons (basis pontis)**: The pons contains ascending and descending tracts, including corticospinal fibers (after they decussate in the medulla). A pontine lesion can cause contralateral hemiparesis and sensory loss, but it would also likely involve cranial nerve nuclei (e.g., CN V, VI, VII, VIII), leading to additional deficits like facial paralysis, diplopia, or hearing loss, which are not described in this patient. Furthermore, the infarct is located in the left cerebral hemisphere, not the brainstem.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                 | Location                                   | Function                                                                 | Lesion Symptoms                                                                                                | Relation to Vignette                                                                                                                               |
| :------------------------ | :----------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Internal Capsule (Posterior Limb)** | White matter, between thalamus & basal ganglia | Contains corticospinal tract (motor), thalamocortical radiations (sensory) | Contralateral hemiparesis (motor), contralateral sensory loss, homonymous hemianopia (if optic radiations involved) | **MOST LIKELY** affected structure causing motor deficits given cortical involvement and pathway location. Consistent with MCA stroke pattern. |
| Basal Ganglia (Putamen)   | Deep cerebral hemisphere                  | Motor control, learning, habit formation                                  | Movement disorders (Parkinsonism, chorea, dystonia, hemiballismus)                                             | Less likely to cause pure hemiparesis with sensory loss and visual field defect.                                                                 |
| Thalamus (VPL)            | Deep cerebral hemisphere                  | Sensory relay station                                                     | Contralateral sensory loss                                                                                     | Explains sensory loss but not motor deficits.                                                                                                     |
| Cerebellum (Left)         | Posterior fossa                            | Coordination, balance, motor learning                                     | Ataxia, dysmetria, intention tremor, nystagmus                                                                | Does not cause hemiparesis or sensory loss.                                                                                                     |
| Pons (Basis Pontis)        | Brainstem                                 | Contains descending motor tracts, cranial nerve nuclei                   | Contralateral hemiparesis/sensory loss, cranial nerve palsies                                                  | Location incorrect (brainstem vs. cerebrum). Cranial nerve deficits absent.                                                                     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The most common cause of contralateral hemiparesis following a stroke is damage to the corticospinal tract, either in the motor cortex (precentral gyrus) or as it descends through the internal capsule. MCA strokes frequently affect both the cortex and the adjacent white matter, including the internal capsule. Differentiating lesions based on location (cortex vs. internal capsule vs. brainstem) is crucial for understanding the specific neurological deficits. Remember that the internal capsule is a critical relay station for both motor and sensory pathways.

<a id='question-023'></a>

### Question 23: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 23 (convoluted)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the specific cortical areas and their functions, particularly those related to language processing (Broca's area, Wernicke's area) and their vascular supply (Middle Cerebral Artery - MCA), and correlating clinical deficits with specific anatomical locations. (理解皮层特定区域及其功能，特别是与语言处理相关的区域（布罗卡区、韦尼克区）及其血管供应（大脑中动脉-MCA），并将临床表现与特定解剖位置相关联。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old right-handed male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with acute onset of difficulty speaking. He reports that he woke up this morning unable to form coherent sentences, although he understands everything spoken to him. His wife confirms that he is using single words and short phrases but cannot construct grammatically correct sentences or follow complex commands. On examination, his vital signs are stable. Neurological examination reveals intact strength and sensation in all extremities. Cranial nerve examination is normal except for the speech deficit. He can repeat simple phrases but struggles with more complex ones. He appears frustrated but oriented to person, place, and time. A non-contrast head CT scan shows no evidence of hemorrhage. An MRI scan performed 24 hours later reveals an acute ischemic infarct in the left hemisphere.

Which of the following areas is most likely affected by the infarct?

(A) Primary motor cortex (Area 4)
(B) Primary somatosensory cortex (Area 3, 1, 2)
(C) Broca's area (Area 44/45)
(D) Wernicke's area (Area 22)
(E) Visual cortex (Area 17)

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old right-handed male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia). This increases the likelihood of a stroke.
- **Chief Complaint:** Acute onset difficulty speaking. This points towards a neurological event affecting language centers.
- **Speech Deficit Characterization:** Difficulty forming coherent sentences ("expressive aphasia"), understands spoken language, can repeat simple phrases but struggles with complex ones. This specific pattern is highly suggestive of Broca's aphasia.
- **Neurological Examination:** Intact strength and sensation, normal cranial nerves (except speech). This localizes the lesion to the language areas, sparing motor and sensory pathways controlling limbs and face, and other cranial nerves.
- **Imaging:** Acute ischemic infarct in the left hemisphere. The left hemisphere is dominant for language in most right-handed individuals.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the most likely affected area based on the clinical presentation.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Language Centers:** The primary language centers are located in the left hemisphere (in most individuals).
    - **Broca's Area:** Located in the inferior frontal gyrus (pars opercularis and pars triangularis) of the dominant hemisphere (typically left). Brodmann areas 44 (pars opercularis) and 45 (pars triangularis). Responsible for speech production and grammar. Damage leads to expressive aphasia (Broca's aphasia), characterized by difficulty producing fluent speech, grammatical errors, and effortful articulation, while comprehension is relatively preserved.
    - **Wernicke's Area:** Located in the posterior superior temporal gyrus of the dominant hemisphere (typically left). Brodmann area 22. Responsible for language comprehension. Damage leads to receptive aphasia (Wernicke's aphasia), characterized by fluent but nonsensical speech (word salad), impaired comprehension, and difficulty repeating.
    - **Arcuate Fasciculus:** Connects Broca's and Wernicke's areas. Damage leads to conduction aphasia, characterized by impaired repetition despite relatively preserved comprehension and production.
- **Vascular Supply:** The language areas are primarily supplied by branches of the Middle Cerebral Artery (MCA). The inferior frontal gyrus (Broca's area) and superior temporal gyrus (Wernicke's area) are within the territory of the MCA.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient presents with expressive aphasia (difficulty producing fluent speech, grammatical errors) with preserved comprehension. This clinical picture strongly points to damage in Broca's area. The infarct is located in the left hemisphere, consistent with the typical location of language centers in a right-handed individual.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Primary motor cortex (Area 4):** Located in the precentral gyrus. Controls voluntary movements. Damage causes contralateral hemiparesis or hemiplegia. The patient has intact strength, ruling this out.
- (B) **Primary somatosensory cortex (Area 3, 1, 2):** Located in the postcentral gyrus. Processes touch, temperature, pain, and proprioception. Damage causes contralateral sensory loss. The patient has intact sensation, ruling this out.
- (D) **Wernicke's area (Area 22):** Located in the posterior superior temporal gyrus. Damage causes receptive aphasia (Wernicke's aphasia), characterized by fluent but nonsensical speech and impaired comprehension. The patient understands language, ruling this out.
- (E) **Visual cortex (Area 17):** Located in the occipital lobe. Processes visual information. Damage causes visual field defects (hemianopia, quadrantanopia). The patient's symptoms are related to language, not vision, ruling this out.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                 | Location                                  | Function                                     | Clinical Deficit (Lesion)                                  | Brodmann Area(s) |
| :------------------------ | :---------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------------- | :--------------- |
| **Broca's Area (Correct)** | Inferior Frontal Gyrus (Left)             | Speech Production, Grammar                   | Expressive Aphasia (non-fluent speech, impaired grammar) | 44, 45           |
| Wernicke's Area           | Posterior Superior Temporal Gyrus (Left) | Language Comprehension                       | Receptive Aphasia (fluent but nonsensical speech, poor comprehension) | 22               |
| Primary Motor Cortex      | Precentral Gyrus                           | Voluntary Movement                            | Contralateral Hemiparesis/Hemiplegia                      | 4                |
| Primary Somatosensory Cortex | Postcentral Gyrus                          | Sensory Perception                            | Contralateral Sensory Loss                                | 3, 1, 2          |
| Visual Cortex             | Occipital Lobe                             | Visual Processing                             | Visual Field Defects                                       | 17               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The clinical presentation of expressive aphasia (difficulty producing fluent speech, grammatical errors) with preserved comprehension, coupled with a left hemisphere infarct in a right-handed individual, strongly indicates damage to Broca's area (Brodmann areas 44 and 45) located in the inferior frontal gyrus. Differentiating between Broca's aphasia (expressive) and Wernicke's aphasia (receptive) is crucial. Remember that motor cortex lesions cause weakness, sensory cortex lesions cause sensory loss, and visual cortex lesions cause visual deficits.
