### Question 24: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 24 (bulges)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Identify the specific cortical area and its associated functional deficit based on a clinical presentation involving sensory and motor changes, correlating with the known cytoarchitecture and functional localization of the cerebral cortex, specifically focusing on the precentral gyrus and postcentral gyrus (大脑皮层第一部分重点24：隆起).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with acute onset of right-sided weakness and numbness. The symptoms began approximately 2 hours prior to arrival. Neurological examination reveals significant weakness in the left upper and lower extremities (strength 2/5), decreased sensation to light touch and pinprick on the left side of the body, and a positive Babinski sign on the left. Cranial nerves are intact, and cognitive function appears grossly preserved based on initial assessment. Non-contrast head CT reveals a hypodense area in the right parietal lobe, suggestive of an acute ischemic stroke. Given the location of the lesion, which specific cortical area is most likely affected, leading to the observed contralateral sensory and motor deficits?

(A) Superior Temporal Gyrus (STG)
(B) Middle Frontal Gyrus (MFG)
(C) Postcentral Gyrus
(D) Inferior Parietal Lobule
(E) Precentral Gyrus

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia) presenting with acute onset right-sided weakness and numbness. This points towards a stroke affecting the contralateral (left) side of the brain.
- **Neurological Examination:** Left-sided weakness (hemiparesis) and decreased sensation (hemianesthesia) indicate damage to motor and sensory pathways originating or passing through the left cerebral hemisphere. The positive Babinski sign confirms upper motor neuron involvement.
- **Imaging:** Non-contrast head CT shows a hypodense area (infarct) in the *right* parietal lobe. This is crucial – the symptoms are on the *left* side, meaning the lesion is on the *right* side of the brain.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the specific cortical area affected, leading to the observed contralateral sensory and motor deficits, given the lesion location in the right parietal lobe.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Cerebral Cortex Localization:** The cerebral cortex is divided into lobes (frontal, parietal, temporal, occipital) and further subdivided into gyri (ridges) and sulci (grooves). Specific functions are localized to certain areas.
- **Motor Cortex:** The primary motor cortex, responsible for voluntary movement, is located in the precentral gyrus (Brodmann area 4), situated immediately anterior to the central sulcus. Lesions here cause contralateral weakness (hemiparesis/hemiplegia).
- **Sensory Cortex:** The primary somatosensory cortex, responsible for processing sensory information (touch, pain, temperature, proprioception), is located in the postcentral gyrus (Brodmann areas 3, 1, 2), situated immediately posterior to the central sulcus. Lesions here cause contralateral sensory loss (hemianesthesia).
- **Central Sulcus:** This prominent sulcus separates the frontal lobe (containing the precentral gyrus) from the parietal lobe (containing the postcentral gyrus).
- **Parietal Lobe:** The parietal lobe, posterior to the central sulcus, contains the postcentral gyrus and the superior and inferior parietal lobules. It is involved in sensory processing, spatial awareness, and integration of sensory information.
- **Vignette Correlation:** The patient has a lesion in the *right* parietal lobe causing *left*-sided sensory and motor deficits. The postcentral gyrus is located in the parietal lobe and is responsible for sensory processing. The precentral gyrus is located in the frontal lobe, just anterior to the central sulcus, and is responsible for motor function. Since the lesion is in the right parietal lobe and causes both sensory and motor deficits on the contralateral (left) side, the postcentral gyrus (sensory) and potentially the adjacent precentral gyrus (motor) are likely involved. The question asks for the cortical area most likely affected leading to *both* deficits, and the postcentral gyrus is definitively within the described lesion location (right parietal lobe) and directly responsible for the sensory deficits. While the precentral gyrus is adjacent and could also be involved, the postcentral gyrus is explicitly located within the parietal lobe mentioned in the CT findings and is the primary site for sensory processing deficits. Therefore, the postcentral gyrus is the most fitting answer given the location and sensory deficits.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Superior Temporal Gyrus (STG):** Located in the temporal lobe, primarily involved in auditory processing (primary auditory cortex) and language comprehension (Wernicke's area). Lesions typically cause auditory deficits or receptive aphasia, not contralateral sensory and motor deficits.
- (B) **Middle Frontal Gyrus (MFG):** Located in the frontal lobe, involved in executive functions, working memory, and motor planning (part of the premotor cortex). Lesions can cause contralateral weakness (if involving the motor cortex or premotor areas) and executive dysfunction, but typically not primary sensory loss. The lesion is described in the parietal lobe, not the frontal lobe.
- (D) **Inferior Parietal Lobule:** Located in the parietal lobe, posterior to the postcentral gyrus. It includes areas like the angular gyrus and supramarginal gyrus, involved in language processing, spatial cognition, and sensory integration. While lesions here can cause sensory deficits and neglect syndromes, they are less directly associated with the primary motor cortex function compared to the precentral gyrus, and the primary sensory cortex is the postcentral gyrus. The postcentral gyrus is the primary cortical area for sensory processing located in the parietal lobe.
- (E) **Precentral Gyrus:** Located in the frontal lobe, immediately anterior to the central sulcus. It contains the primary motor cortex. Lesions cause contralateral weakness (hemiparesis/hemiplegia). While the patient has weakness, the lesion is described as being in the *parietal* lobe, and the question asks for the area responsible for *both* sensory and motor deficits. The postcentral gyrus is in the parietal lobe and responsible for sensory deficits. Although the precentral gyrus is adjacent and could be affected, the postcentral gyrus fits the location (parietal lobe) and explains the sensory deficits directly.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                 | Location         | Primary Function(s)                                  | Lesion Deficit(s)                                  | USMLE Relevance                                                                 |
| :------------------------ | :--------------- | :--------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| **Postcentral Gyrus (C)** | Parietal Lobe    | Primary Somatosensory Cortex (Touch, Pain, Temp, Proprioception) | Contralateral Sensory Loss (Hemianesthesia)         | **Correct Answer:** Located in the parietal lobe, explains sensory deficits.      |
| Precentral Gyrus (E)      | Frontal Lobe     | Primary Motor Cortex                                   | Contralateral Weakness (Hemiparesis/Hemiplegia)     | Adjacent to postcentral gyrus, explains motor deficits, but not in parietal lobe. |
| Superior Temporal Gyrus (A) | Temporal Lobe    | Auditory Processing, Language Comprehension (Wernicke's) | Auditory Agnosia, Receptive Aphasia                 | Different lobe, different function.                                             |
| Middle Frontal Gyrus (B)  | Frontal Lobe     | Executive Function, Motor Planning (Premotor Cortex)   | Weakness, Executive Dysfunction                     | Different lobe, different function.                                             |
| Inferior Parietal Lobule (D) | Parietal Lobe    | Sensory Integration, Spatial Cognition, Language      | Sensory Deficits, Neglect, Gerstmann Syndrome       | Located in parietal lobe, but less primary for sensory processing than postcentral gyrus. |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The primary motor cortex is located in the precentral gyrus (frontal lobe), and the primary somatosensory cortex is located in the postcentral gyrus (parietal lobe), separated by the central sulcus. Lesions in these areas cause contralateral motor and sensory deficits, respectively. Understanding the precise location of cortical areas within lobes and their functions is crucial for localizing neurological deficits. Key differentials include other parietal lobe structures (inferior parietal lobule) and frontal lobe structures (precentral gyrus, MFG), as well as temporal lobe structures (STG). Always correlate the lesion location with the observed deficits.

<a id='question-025'></a>

### Question 025: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 25 (eminenc)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Identify the specific cortical area and its associated vascular territory based on a complex neurological deficit pattern, understanding the functional specialization and blood supply of the cerebral cortex. (鉴别复杂的神经功能缺损模式，确定特定的皮质区域及其血管供应区域，理解大脑皮质的功能特化和血供。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with acute onset of right-sided weakness and sensory loss. The weakness predominantly affects the right face and arm, with relative sparing of the leg. He also exhibits expressive aphasia (difficulty speaking) and right facial droop. Neurological examination reveals right hemiparesis (face > arm > leg), right hemisensory loss (face > arm > leg), right lower facial weakness (central type), and impaired speech production with preserved comprehension. MRI of the brain shows an acute ischemic infarct in the left hemisphere.

Which cortical area is most likely affected by this infarct?

(A) Left primary motor cortex (Brodmann area 4)
(B) Left primary somatosensory cortex (Brodmann area 3, 1, 2)
(C) Left premotor cortex and supplementary motor area (Brodmann area 6)
(D) Left inferior frontal gyrus, including Broca's area and the anterior portion of the precentral gyrus (Brodmann areas 44, 45, and anterior 4)
(E) Left parietal lobe, including the postcentral gyrus (Brodmann areas 3, 1, 2) and superior parietal lobule (Brodmann areas 5, 7)

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia) suggests a stroke is likely.
- **Acute Onset:** Right-sided weakness and sensory loss point to a left hemisphere lesion.
- **Motor Deficit:** Right hemiparesis (face > arm > leg) indicates involvement of the motor cortex or its descending pathways. The face and arm involvement > leg involvement is characteristic of a lesion affecting the lateral aspect of the motor cortex, where the leg area is located more medially.
- **Sensory Deficit:** Right hemisensory loss (face > arm > leg) mirrors the motor deficit pattern, suggesting involvement of the somatosensory cortex or its ascending pathways.
- **Facial Weakness:** Right lower facial weakness (central type) implies upper motor neuron involvement, consistent with a cortical lesion.
- **Aphasia:** Expressive aphasia (difficulty speaking, preserved comprehension) points to damage in Broca's area, located in the inferior frontal gyrus.
- **MRI:** Confirms an acute ischemic infarct in the left hemisphere.
- **Synthesis:** The combination of right face/arm > leg motor and sensory deficits, right lower facial weakness, and expressive aphasia strongly localizes the lesion to the left hemisphere, specifically involving the anterior portion of the precentral gyrus (motor cortex for face/arm) and the inferior frontal gyrus (Broca's area).

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Cerebral Cortex Organization:** The cerebral cortex is divided into lobes (frontal, parietal, temporal, occipital) and functional areas (motor, sensory, association). Brodmann areas are cytoarchitectural regions.
- **Motor Cortex (Brodmann Area 4):** Located in the precentral gyrus. Controls voluntary movement. Organized somatotopically (motor homunculus). The leg area is most medial, followed by trunk, arm, hand, face. A lesion affecting the lateral part (face/arm representation) causes face/arm weakness > leg weakness.
- **Somatosensory Cortex (Brodmann Areas 3, 1, 2):** Located in the postcentral gyrus. Receives sensory information. Also organized somatotopically, mirroring the motor homunculus. A lesion affecting the lateral part causes face/arm sensory loss > leg sensory loss.
- **Premotor Cortex & SMA (Brodmann Area 6):** Located anterior to the primary motor cortex. Involved in planning and sequencing movements. Lesions typically cause apraxia or impaired motor planning, not the specific sensory/motor pattern described.
- **Inferior Frontal Gyrus:** Contains Broca's area (Brodmann areas 44, 45) responsible for speech production, and the anterior portion of the precentral gyrus (part of Brodmann area 4) representing the face and arm.
- **Parietal Lobe:** Contains the primary somatosensory cortex (postcentral gyrus) and association areas. Lesions cause sensory deficits, spatial neglect (if right parietal), or Gerstmann syndrome (if dominant parietal).
- **Vascular Supply:** The anterior cerebral artery (ACA) supplies the medial aspects of the frontal and parietal lobes (leg area of motor/sensory cortex). The middle cerebral artery (MCA) supplies the lateral aspects of the frontal, parietal, and temporal lobes (face/arm area of motor/sensory cortex, Broca's area, Wernicke's area). The posterior cerebral artery (PCA) supplies the occipital lobe and inferior temporal lobe.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's symptoms (right face/arm > leg motor/sensory deficits, expressive aphasia) are classic for a lesion affecting the lateral aspect of the left hemisphere, involving both the motor cortex (face/arm representation) and Broca's area. This territory is primarily supplied by the MCA. Option (D) accurately describes this anatomical region (inferior frontal gyrus including Broca's area and the anterior portion of the precentral gyrus).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Left primary motor cortex (Brodmann area 4) - This is partially correct as it includes the motor cortex, but it doesn't account for the expressive aphasia (Broca's area involvement). The lesion involves *both* the motor cortex (face/arm part) and Broca's area.
- (B): Left primary somatosensory cortex (Brodmann area 3, 1, 2) - This is also partially correct as it includes the sensory cortex, but it doesn't account for the motor deficits or the expressive aphasia. The lesion involves *both* the sensory cortex (face/arm part) and Broca's area.
- (C): Left premotor cortex and supplementary motor area (Brodmann area 6) - Lesions here typically cause apraxia, difficulty with complex motor sequences, or contralateral neglect, not the specific face/arm > leg motor/sensory deficits and expressive aphasia seen in this patient.
- (E): Left parietal lobe, including the postcentral gyrus (Brodmann areas 3, 1, 2) and superior parietal lobule (Brodmann areas 5, 7) - This includes the somatosensory cortex, but a lesion limited to the parietal lobe would not typically cause expressive aphasia (Broca's area is in the frontal lobe) or the specific motor deficits involving the face and arm more than the leg.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure/Area                 | Brodmann Area(s) | Location                                  | Primary Function(s)                                  | Clinical Deficit (Left Lesion)                                                                 | Vascular Supply (Typical) |
| :----------------------------- | :--------------- | :---------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------ |
| **Correct Answer (D)**         | 44, 45, 4 (ant)  | Inferior Frontal Gyrus, Ant. Precentral Gyrus | Speech Production (Broca's), Face/Arm Motor Control | Expressive Aphasia, Right Face/Arm > Leg Weakness/Sensory Loss, Right Lower Facial Weakness | MCA                      |
| (A) Primary Motor Cortex       | 4                | Precentral Gyrus                          | Voluntary Movement                                   | Right Face/Arm > Leg Weakness                                                                  | MCA                      |
| (B) Primary Somatosensory Cortex | 3, 1, 2          | Postcentral Gyrus                         | Sensory Perception                                   | Right Face/Arm > Leg Sensory Loss                                                               | MCA                      |
| (C) Premotor/SMA               | 6                | Anterior to Precentral Gyrus              | Motor Planning, Sequencing                            | Apraxia, Impaired Motor Planning                                                               | MCA                      |
| (E) Parietal Lobe              | 3, 1, 2, 5, 7    | Posterior to Central Sulcus               | Somatosensation, Spatial Awareness, Integration      | Sensory Loss, Neglect (Right Lesion), Gerstmann Syndrome (Dominant Lesion)                     | MCA, ACA                  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The combination of contralateral face/arm > leg motor and sensory deficits with expressive aphasia strongly points to a lesion involving the lateral aspect of the dominant hemisphere's frontal lobe, specifically the inferior frontal gyrus (Broca's area) and the anterior portion of the precentral gyrus (face/arm motor cortex). This area is typically supplied by the middle cerebral artery (MCA). Differentiating between cortical areas based on specific neurological deficits is crucial for stroke localization. Remember the somatotopic organization of the motor and sensory cortices (leg medial, face lateral) and the functions of Broca's (speech production) and Wernicke's (speech comprehension) areas.

<a id='question-026'></a>

### Question 26: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 26 (referred)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the functional localization within the cerebral cortex, specifically the relationship between the primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus) and its corresponding deep structures (internal capsule), and the clinical manifestations of lesions affecting these areas, particularly related to upper limb motor control. (理解大脑皮层的功能定位，特别是初级运动皮层（中央前回）与其深部结构（内囊）的关系，以及损伤这些区域的临床表现，特别是与上肢运动控制相关的表现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old right-handed male presents to the emergency department with acute onset of right-sided weakness, predominantly affecting his right arm and face, but sparing his right leg. He reports difficulty lifting his right arm and has noticed drooling from the right side of his mouth. His speech is slightly slurred, but he can understand and respond appropriately. Past medical history is significant for hypertension and hyperlipidemia, both poorly controlled. Neurological examination reveals right facial droop (lower face), right arm weakness (proximal > distal, 3/5 strength), and right hand weakness (intrinsic muscles, 2/5 strength). Right leg strength is 5/5. Sensory examination is intact bilaterally. Reflexes are normal and symmetric. A non-contrast head CT scan shows no evidence of hemorrhage. An MRI scan performed 24 hours later reveals an acute ischemic infarct in the left cerebral hemisphere.

Which of the following structures is MOST likely affected by the infarct, leading to the patient's specific motor deficits?

(A) Left superior parietal lobule
(B) Left internal capsule (posterior limb)
(C) Left precentral gyrus (motor strip for leg)
(D) Left basal ganglia (caudate nucleus)
(E) Left cerebellum (inferior hemisphere)

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old right-handed male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia). This suggests a potential stroke.
- **Onset:** Acute onset of right-sided weakness (hemiparesis).
- **Distribution of Weakness:** Right arm and face > Right leg. This pattern is crucial. The face and arm are represented medially in the motor cortex and the internal capsule, while the leg is represented more laterally. The sparing of the leg points towards a lesion affecting the medial portion of the motor pathway.
- **Specific Deficits:** Right facial droop (lower face, indicating upper motor neuron lesion), right arm weakness (proximal > distal, intrinsic hand muscles affected), slurred speech (dysarthria, potentially due to facial/tongue muscle weakness).
- **Sensory:** Intact sensation rules out lesions primarily affecting sensory pathways.
- **Imaging:** MRI confirms acute ischemic infarct in the left hemisphere. Since the weakness is on the right side, the lesion must be in the left hemisphere.
- **Question:** Asks for the MOST likely affected structure causing this specific pattern of motor deficit.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Motor Cortex (Precentral Gyrus):** Located in the frontal lobe (Brodmann areas 4 and 6). Controls voluntary movement. Organized somatotopically (motor homunculus). The medial part controls the face and arm, the lateral part controls the trunk and leg. A lesion confined to the leg area of the precentral gyrus would cause leg weakness, not the observed pattern.
- **Internal Capsule:** A large white matter structure deep within the cerebral hemispheres, situated between the thalamus and the basal ganglia. It contains ascending sensory fibers (thalamocortical radiations) and descending motor fibers (corticospinal, corticobulbar, corticopontine tracts). The motor fibers are arranged somatotopically within the internal capsule. The posterior limb contains fibers for the face, arm, and trunk, arranged medially to laterally. The anterior limb contains fibers for the leg and foot.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's right-sided weakness affecting the face and arm, but sparing the leg, strongly suggests a lesion affecting the medial portion of the descending motor pathways *after* they have left the motor cortex. This specific pattern is characteristic of a lesion in the medial part of the posterior limb of the left internal capsule. The corticobulbar tract fibers (controlling facial muscles) and the corticospinal tract fibers for the arm run through this medial region. The fibers for the leg run more laterally in the anterior limb or the posterior limb.
- **Other Structures:**
    - **Superior Parietal Lobule:** Primarily involved in sensory integration, spatial awareness, and attention. Lesions typically cause sensory deficits, neglect, or apraxia, not this specific motor pattern.
    - **Basal Ganglia (Caudate Nucleaus):** Involved in motor control, planning, and execution, but lesions typically cause movement disorders like Parkinsonism, Huntington's disease, or hemiballismus, not isolated hemiparesis with this specific distribution.
    - **Cerebellum (Inferior Hemisphere):** Primarily involved in coordination, balance, and motor learning. Lesions typically cause ataxia, dysmetria, intention tremor, and dysdiadochokinesia, not isolated hemiparesis.

##### 3. Embryological Origin:**
- The cerebral cortex develops from the neural tube (neuroectoderm).
- The internal capsule develops from the white matter tracts originating from the cortex and thalamus.
- The basal ganglia develop from the telencephalon.
- The cerebellum develops from the rhombencephalon.
- The parietal lobule develops from the telencephalon.

##### 4. Vascular Supply:**
- The motor cortex and internal capsule are primarily supplied by the Middle Cerebral Artery (MCA). Occlusion of the MCA or its branches is a common cause of stroke affecting these areas.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Left superior parietal lobule:** Incorrect. Lesions here typically cause contralateral sensory loss, astereognosis, spatial neglect (if right parietal lobe), or Gerstmann syndrome (if dominant parietal lobe). They do not typically cause isolated motor deficits affecting the face and arm more than the leg.
- (C) **Left precentral gyrus (motor strip for leg):** Incorrect. A lesion confined to the leg area of the precentral gyrus would cause weakness primarily in the contralateral leg, which is spared in this patient. The patient's deficits involve the face and arm, which are represented medially in the motor cortex.
- (D) **Left basal ganglia (caudate nucleus):** Incorrect. Lesions of the basal ganglia typically cause movement disorders (e.g., Parkinsonism, chorea, dystonia, hemiballismus) rather than isolated hemiparesis with a specific somatotopic distribution. While basal ganglia lesions can affect motor function, the pattern described is not typical.
- (E) **Left cerebellum (inferior hemisphere):** Incorrect. Cerebellar lesions cause problems with coordination, balance, and smooth execution of movements (ataxia, dysmetria, intention tremor). They do not cause isolated weakness (paresis) of the type described.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                     | Location                                  | Primary Function                                  | Typical Lesion Manifestations                                                                 | Relevance to Case                                                                                                                               |
| :--------------------------- | :---------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ | :-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Internal Capsule (Posterior Limb)** | Deep white matter, between thalamus & basal ganglia | Major pathway for descending motor (corticospinal/bulbar) & ascending sensory fibers | Contralateral hemiparesis/hemiplegia (often face/arm > leg if medial), contralateral sensory loss | **Correct.** Medial lesion affects face/arm fibers, sparing leg fibers. Matches patient's presentation.                                             |
| Precentral Gyrus (Motor Cortex) | Frontal lobe, anterior to central sulcus   | Initiation of voluntary movement                   | Contralateral weakness corresponding to the specific cortical area affected (e.g., leg area lesion = leg weakness) | Incorrect. Leg area lesion would cause leg weakness, which is spared. Face/arm area lesion would cause face/arm weakness, but internal capsule lesion is more specific for this *distribution*. |
| Superior Parietal Lobule      | Parietal lobe, posterior to central sulcus   | Sensory integration, spatial awareness, attention | Contralateral sensory loss, neglect, astereognosis, apraxia                                  | Incorrect. Primarily sensory/integrative functions, not this motor pattern.                                                                     |
| Basal Ganglia (Caudate)       | Deep within cerebral hemispheres            | Motor control, planning, habit formation          | Movement disorders (Parkinsonism, chorea, dystonia, hemiballismus)                             | Incorrect. Causes movement disorders, not isolated hemiparesis with specific distribution.                                                         |
| Cerebellum                    | Posterior fossa, inferior to occipital lobes | Coordination, balance, motor learning             | Ataxia, dysmetria, intention tremor, dysdiadochokinesia                                       | Incorrect. Causes coordination deficits, not weakness.                                                                                             |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The specific pattern of contralateral hemiparesis affecting the face and arm more severely than the leg is a classic sign of a lesion affecting the medial portion of the posterior limb of the internal capsule. This area contains the corticospinal and corticobulbar tracts for the upper limb and face, which are located medially within the capsule, while the leg fibers are located more laterally. Differentiating this pattern from lesions of the motor cortex (which would affect a specific body part based on cortical representation) or other structures like the basal ganglia or cerebellum is crucial for neurological localization.

<a id='question-027'></a>

### Question 27: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 27 (spaces)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the functional localization within the cerebral cortex, specifically the relationship between the primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus) and the corresponding body parts, and the vascular supply (middle cerebral artery) to this region. (理解大脑皮层的功能定位，特别是初级运动皮层（中央前回）与相应身体部位之间的关系，以及该区域的血管供应（大脑中动脉）。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with acute onset of right-sided weakness and numbness. The symptoms began approximately 2 hours ago while he was watching television. On examination, his vital signs are stable. Neurological examination reveals significant weakness in the left face, arm, and leg (strength 2/5 throughout). Sensory examination shows decreased sensation to light touch and pinprick on the left side of his body, also affecting the face, arm, and leg. Reflexes are hyperactive on the left side, and a positive Babinski sign is present on the left. Cranial nerve examination is otherwise normal, except for mild left facial droop. A non-contrast head CT scan shows no evidence of hemorrhage. An MRI scan performed 6 hours later reveals an acute ischemic infarct in the left cerebral hemisphere.

Which area of the cerebral cortex is most likely affected by this infarct?

(A) Postcentral gyrus
(B) Precentral gyrus
(C) Angular gyrus
(D) Temporal lobe
(E) Occipital lobe

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia) presenting with acute onset right-sided weakness and numbness. This strongly suggests a stroke affecting the contralateral (left) side of the brain.
- **Neurological Examination:**
    - **Right-sided weakness (hemiparesis):** Indicates damage to the motor pathways controlling the right side of the body, originating in the left cerebral hemisphere.
    - **Right-sided numbness (hemianesthesia):** Indicates damage to the sensory pathways carrying information from the right side of the body, originating in the left cerebral hemisphere.
    - **Left facial droop:** Indicates involvement of the facial motor cortex or its descending pathways, located in the left hemisphere.
    - **Hyperreflexia and positive Babinski sign on the left:** Indicate upper motor neuron damage affecting the left side of the body.
- **Imaging:** MRI confirms an acute ischemic infarct in the left cerebral hemisphere.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the specific area of the cerebral cortex most likely affected.
- **Synthesis:** The combination of contralateral (right-sided) motor and sensory deficits affecting the face, arm, and leg points to damage in the primary motor cortex and primary somatosensory cortex, respectively, located in the left hemisphere. The primary motor cortex is responsible for voluntary movement, and the primary somatosensory cortex receives sensory input. Both are located in the frontal and parietal lobes, respectively, adjacent to the central sulcus. The motor cortex is specifically located in the precentral gyrus, just anterior to the central sulcus.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Precentral Gyrus:** Located in the frontal lobe, immediately anterior to the central sulcus. It constitutes the primary motor cortex (Brodmann area 4). It contains large pyramidal neurons (Betz cells) whose axons form the corticospinal tract, responsible for voluntary motor control of the contralateral body. The motor homunculus represents the disproportionate cortical area dedicated to different body parts (e.g., hands, face, tongue have larger representations).
- **Postcentral Gyrus:** Located in the parietal lobe, immediately posterior to the central sulcus. It constitutes the primary somatosensory cortex (Brodmann areas 3, 1, 2). It receives sensory information (touch, pain, temperature, proprioception) from the contralateral body.
- **Angular Gyrus:** Located in the parietal lobe, superior to the temporal lobe and posterior to the postcentral gyrus. It is involved in language processing, number processing, spatial cognition, memory retrieval, attention, and theory of mind. Damage can cause Gerstmann syndrome.
- **Temporal Lobe:** Located inferior to the frontal and parietal lobes. Contains the primary auditory cortex, Wernicke's area (language comprehension), hippocampus (memory), and amygdala (emotion).
- **Occipital Lobe:** Located posterior to the parietal and temporal lobes. Contains the primary visual cortex.
- **Vascular Supply:** The middle cerebral artery (MCA) is the primary blood supply to the lateral surface of the cerebral hemispheres, including the frontal, parietal, and temporal lobes. Occlusion of the MCA or its branches commonly causes strokes affecting the motor and sensory cortex, leading to contralateral hemiparesis and hemisensory loss, as seen in this patient. The precentral and postcentral gyri are particularly vulnerable to MCA territory infarcts.
- **Embryology:** The cerebral cortex develops from the neural tube via neurogenesis and neuronal migration. The six layers of the neocortex are formed through complex processes involving radial glial cells. The specific functional areas (motor, sensory, etc.) are established postnatally through experience and refinement of connections.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's symptoms (contralateral hemiparesis, hemisensory loss, facial droop) are classic signs of a stroke affecting the motor and sensory cortex in the left hemisphere. The precentral gyrus (primary motor cortex) is the most likely site responsible for the motor deficits. The postcentral gyrus (primary somatosensory cortex) is responsible for the sensory deficits. Since the question asks for *an* area affected, and the motor deficits are prominent and localized to the precentral gyrus, this is the most direct answer.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Postcentral gyrus:** This is the primary somatosensory cortex. While damage here would cause contralateral sensory loss (which the patient has), the question asks for *an* area affected, and the motor deficits (weakness, facial droop) are strongly linked to the precentral gyrus. The patient has both motor and sensory deficits, but the precentral gyrus is the primary motor area. A stroke often affects both gyri due to their proximity and shared vascular supply (MCA). However, the precentral gyrus is specifically responsible for the motor output.
- (C) **Angular gyrus:** Located in the parietal lobe, involved in higher-order functions like language and spatial processing. Damage typically causes deficits like Gerstmann syndrome (agraphia, acalculia, finger agnosia, left-right disorientation) or alexia without agraphia, not the primary motor and sensory deficits seen here.
- (D) **Temporal lobe:** Contains auditory cortex, Wernicke's area, hippocampus. Damage causes auditory deficits, language comprehension problems (Wernicke's aphasia), or memory impairment, none of which are the primary features of this patient's presentation.
- (E) **Occipital lobe:** Contains the primary visual cortex. Damage causes visual field defects (hemianopia, quadrantanopia), not motor or sensory deficits of the limbs and face.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure          | Location             | Function                                  | Key Deficit with Lesion                                  | Vascular Supply (Primary) |
|--------------------|----------------------|-------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------|
| Precentral Gyrus   | Frontal Lobe, ant. to Central Sulcus | Primary Motor Cortex (Voluntary Movement) | Contralateral Hemiparesis/Hemiplegia, Facial Weakness | Middle Cerebral Artery   |
| Postcentral Gyrus  | Parietal Lobe, post. to Central Sulcus | Primary Somatosensory Cortex (Sensation) | Contralateral Hemisensory Loss                          | Middle Cerebral Artery   |
| Angular Gyrus      | Parietal Lobe, post. to Temporal Lobe | Language, Spatial Cognition, Number Processing | Gerstmann Syndrome, Alexia without Agraphia             | Middle Cerebral Artery   |
| Temporal Lobe      | Inferior to Frontal/Parietal Lobes | Auditory Processing, Language Comprehension, Memory | Auditory Agnosia, Wernicke's Aphasia, Amnesia           | Middle Cerebral Artery   |
| Occipital Lobe     | Posterior Lobe        | Visual Processing                          | Visual Field Defects (Hemianopia)                         | Posterior Cerebral Artery |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The clinical presentation of contralateral hemiparesis and hemisensory loss strongly localizes the lesion to the motor and sensory cortex in the opposite hemisphere. The precentral gyrus is the primary motor cortex, and the postcentral gyrus is the primary somatosensory cortex. Strokes affecting the MCA territory commonly involve both these areas. Key differentials to consider based on location include lesions in the internal capsule (affecting corticospinal/spinothalamic tracts), thalamus, or brainstem, but the cortical localization is most direct given the specific deficits described and the MRI findings.

<a id='question-028'></a>

### Question 28: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 28 (separating)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical separation of the primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus) and primary somatosensory cortex (postcentral gyrus) by the central sulcus, and the functional significance of this separation in terms of motor execution and sensory perception. (理解运动皮层（中央前回）和感觉皮层（中央感觉区）由中央沟分隔的解剖结构，以及这种分隔在运动执行和感觉知觉方面的功能意义。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department after experiencing sudden onset of right-sided weakness and numbness. He reports difficulty moving his right arm and leg, and decreased sensation on the right side of his body. Neurological examination reveals right hemiparesis (strength 3/5 in the right upper and lower extremities) and right hemisensory loss (decreased light touch and pinprick sensation). Reflexes are hyperactive on the right side. Cranial nerve examination is intact. An emergent non-contrast head CT scan reveals a left middle cerebral artery (MCA) territory infarct. The infarct specifically affects the region of the brain responsible for initiating voluntary movement of the contralateral limbs and trunk, while sparing the region responsible for processing tactile information from the contralateral limbs and trunk.

Which anatomical structure is most likely located *between* the affected motor cortex area and the spared sensory cortex area in this patient?

(A) Parieto-occipital sulcus
(B) Lateral sulcus (Sylvian fissure)
(C) Central sulcus (Rolandic fissure)
(D) Temporal sulcus
(E) Calcarine sulcus

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia) presenting with acute right hemiparesis and hemisensory loss. This strongly suggests a stroke affecting the left cerebral hemisphere.
- **Neurological Exam:** Right hemiparesis (motor deficit) and right hemisensory loss (sensory deficit) indicate involvement of both the motor and sensory cortex controlling the right side of the body. Hyperactive reflexes on the right side are consistent with an upper motor neuron lesion.
- **Imaging:** Left MCA territory infarct. The MCA supplies a large portion of the lateral cerebral hemisphere, including the motor and sensory cortex.
- **Specific Deficit Pattern:** The infarct affects the area responsible for *initiating voluntary movement* (motor cortex) but *spares* the area responsible for *processing tactile information* (sensory cortex).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the anatomical structure separating the affected motor cortex area (precentral gyrus) and the spared sensory cortex area (postcentral gyrus).

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Motor Cortex:** Located in the precentral gyrus of the frontal lobe. Responsible for planning and executing voluntary movements. The left precentral gyrus controls the right side of the body.
- **Sensory Cortex:** Located in the postcentral gyrus of the parietal lobe. Responsible for processing somatosensory information (touch, pain, temperature, proprioception). The left postcentral gyrus receives sensory information from the right side of the body.
- **Central Sulcus (Rolandic Fissure):** A prominent sulcus that separates the frontal lobe (containing the precentral gyrus/motor cortex) from the parietal lobe (containing the postcentral gyrus/sensory cortex). It runs obliquely downwards and forwards from the superior margin of the hemisphere to the lateral sulcus.
- **MCA Stroke:** An MCA stroke often affects both the motor and sensory cortex, leading to contralateral hemiparesis and hemisensory loss, as seen in this patient. The question specifies that the motor area is affected while the sensory area is spared, implying a lesion localized within the MCA territory but specifically involving the precentral gyrus more severely or exclusively than the postcentral gyrus, or perhaps a specific branch of the MCA supplying the precentral gyrus. Regardless, the anatomical separation between these two functional areas is the central sulcus.
- **Embryology:** The cerebral cortex develops from the neural tube. The frontal and parietal lobes, including the motor and sensory cortices, arise from the telencephalon. The central sulcus forms during development as the brain folds and invaginates.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Parieto-occipital sulcus:** Separates the parietal lobe from the occipital lobe. The occipital lobe is primarily involved in visual processing. This sulcus is posterior to the central sulcus and not directly related to the separation between motor and sensory cortex.
- (B) **Lateral sulcus (Sylvian fissure):** Separates the temporal lobe from the frontal and parietal lobes. It is located inferiorly and laterally to the central sulcus. While the MCA runs through this sulcus, it does not separate the motor and sensory cortices.
- (D) **Temporal sulcus:** Located within the temporal lobe, separating the superior temporal gyrus from the middle temporal gyrus. It is irrelevant to the separation between the frontal and parietal lobes.
- (E) **Calcarine sulcus:** Located on the medial surface of the occipital lobe, surrounding the primary visual cortex (V1). It is involved in visual processing and is located far from the motor and sensory cortices.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                 | Location                                  | Separates                                                                 | Functionally Related To                                                                 |
| :------------------------ | :---------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Central Sulcus (C)**     | Between frontal and parietal lobes        | Frontal lobe (Motor Cortex) from Parietal lobe (Sensory Cortex)            | **Motor vs. Sensory Cortex**                                                            |
| Parieto-occipital sulcus (A) | Between parietal and occipital lobes      | Parietal lobe from Occipital lobe                                          | Somatosensory association vs. Visual processing                                         |
| Lateral sulcus (B)        | Between temporal and other lobes          | Temporal lobe from Frontal and Parietal lobes                               | Auditory, visual, memory processing; MCA location                                       |
| Temporal sulcus (D)       | Within the temporal lobe                  | Superior temporal gyrus from Middle temporal gyrus                          | Auditory processing, language comprehension (Wernicke's area)                           |
| Calcarine sulcus (E)      | Medial occipital lobe                     | Surrounds primary visual cortex (V1)                                       | Visual processing                                                                      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The central sulcus is the key anatomical landmark separating the primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus) in the frontal lobe from the primary somatosensory cortex (postcentral gyrus) in the parietal lobe. Strokes affecting the MCA territory can involve both these areas, but the central sulcus defines their boundary. Remember the location of the central sulcus relative to other major sulci like the lateral sulcus and the parieto-occipital sulcus. Key differentials include the lateral sulcus (MCA location, separates temporal lobe), parieto-occipital sulcus (separates parietal/occipital lobes), and temporal sulcus (within temporal lobe).

<a id='question-029'></a>

### Question 029: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 29 (called)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the functional localization within the cerebral cortex, specifically the relationship between the primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus) and the corresponding body parts, and the vascular supply (middle cerebral artery) to this region. (理解大脑皮层的功能定位，特别是初级运动皮层（中央前回）与相应身体部位之间的关系，以及该区域的血管供应（大脑中动脉）。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old right-handed male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with sudden onset of weakness and numbness on his left side. The symptoms began approximately 3 hours ago while he was watching television. Neurological examination reveals significant weakness in the left upper and lower extremities, particularly affecting the left face and arm (estimated strength 2/5). Sensory examination shows decreased sensation to light touch and pinprick on the left side of his body, also more pronounced on the face and arm. He exhibits left facial droop and difficulty articulating speech (dysarthria). Reflexes are hyperactive on the left side. Cranial nerve examination is otherwise normal, including intact vision and eye movements. A non-contrast head CT scan shows no evidence of hemorrhage. An MRI scan performed 6 hours later reveals an acute ischemic infarct in the right cerebral hemisphere.

Which area of the cerebral cortex is most likely affected by this infarct?

(A) Postcentral gyrus
(B) Precentral gyrus
(C) Angular gyrus
(D) Temporal lobe
(E) Occipital lobe

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia). This increases the likelihood of a stroke.
- **Onset:** Sudden onset of left-sided weakness and numbness suggests a vascular event (stroke).
- **Symptoms:** Left-sided weakness (hemiparesis) and numbness (hemianesthesia), particularly affecting the face and arm, indicate a lesion in the contralateral (right) cerebral hemisphere affecting motor and sensory pathways.
- **Facial Droop & Dysarthria:** Involvement of the face and speech muscles points towards involvement of the cortical areas controlling these functions, located in the lateral aspect of the motor cortex.
- **Hyperactive Reflexes:** Suggests an upper motor neuron lesion, consistent with a cortical stroke.
- **Imaging:** MRI confirms an acute ischemic infarct in the right cerebral hemisphere.
- **Question:** Asks for the specific area of the cerebral cortex affected.

The combination of contralateral hemiparesis (especially face and arm > leg), hemisensory loss, facial droop, and dysarthria strongly points to damage in the primary motor cortex and adjacent sensory cortex located in the right hemisphere. The precentral gyrus is the location of the primary motor cortex.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Precentral Gyrus:** Located in the frontal lobe, immediately anterior to the central sulcus. It constitutes the primary motor cortex (Brodmann area 4).
- **Function:** Controls voluntary movements of the contralateral body. The motor homunculus represents the disproportionate cortical area dedicated to different body parts (face and hands have large representations).
- **Lesion Effects:** Damage to the precentral gyrus results in contralateral weakness (hemiparesis) or paralysis (hemiplegia). The specific body parts affected depend on the location of the lesion within the gyrus. A lesion affecting the lateral aspect (controlling face and arm) would cause weakness predominantly in these areas, as seen in the patient.
- **Postcentral Gyrus:** Located posterior to the central sulcus. It constitutes the primary somatosensory cortex (Brodmann areas 3, 1, 2). It receives sensory information from the contralateral body. Lesions cause contralateral sensory loss. The patient has sensory loss, indicating potential involvement of the postcentral gyrus, but the prominent motor deficits (weakness, facial droop, dysarthria) make the precentral gyrus the primary site. Often, strokes affect both gyri due to their proximity and shared vascular supply.
- **Angular Gyrus:** Located in the parietal lobe, part of the association cortex. Involved in language processing, number processing, spatial cognition, memory retrieval, attention, and theory of mind. Lesions can cause Gerstmann syndrome (agraphia, acalculia, finger agnosia, left-right disorientation). Not consistent with the patient's presentation.
- **Temporal Lobe:** Involved in auditory processing, memory formation (hippocampus), language comprehension (Wernicke's area). Lesions can cause auditory hallucinations, memory deficits, or receptive aphasia. Not consistent with the patient's presentation.
- **Occipital Lobe:** Primarily involved in visual processing. Lesions cause visual field defects. Not consistent with the patient's presentation.
- **Vascular Supply:** The precentral and postcentral gyri are primarily supplied by the middle cerebral artery (MCA), particularly its superior division. An MCA stroke is a common cause of contralateral hemiparesis and hemisensory loss, often affecting the face and arm more than the leg. This aligns with the patient's presentation and the MRI findings of an infarct in the right hemisphere.
- **Embryology:** The cerebral cortex develops from the neural tube via neurogenesis and neuronal migration. The frontal lobe, including the precentral gyrus, develops from the anterior neural tube.

##### 3. Why Precentral Gyrus (B) is Correct
The patient's symptoms (contralateral hemiparesis affecting face and arm, facial droop, dysarthria, contralateral hemisensory loss) are classic signs of a stroke affecting the motor and sensory cortex in the contralateral hemisphere. The precentral gyrus houses the primary motor cortex, responsible for initiating voluntary movements. Damage here directly explains the motor deficits. The adjacent postcentral gyrus (sensory cortex) is also likely involved, explaining the sensory deficits. Given the prominence of motor signs (weakness, facial droop, dysarthria), the precentral gyrus is the most likely primary area affected.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Postcentral Gyrus:** This is the primary somatosensory cortex. While the patient has sensory loss, the motor symptoms (weakness, facial droop, dysarthria) are more prominent and directly point to the motor cortex. Strokes often affect both gyri, but the question asks for the *most likely* area given the constellation of symptoms, where motor deficits are dominant. A lesion solely in the postcentral gyrus would primarily cause sensory loss without significant motor weakness.
- (C) **Angular Gyrus:** Located in the parietal lobe, involved in higher-order cognitive functions like language and spatial processing. Lesions here cause different deficits (e.g., Gerstmann syndrome), not contralateral hemiparesis and hemisensory loss.
- (D) **Temporal Lobe:** Primarily involved in auditory processing, memory, and language comprehension. Lesions cause deficits related to these functions (e.g., auditory hallucinations, memory loss, Wernicke's aphasia), not contralateral motor/sensory deficits.
- (E) **Occipital Lobe:** Primarily involved in visual processing. Lesions cause visual field defects (hemianopia, quadrantanopia), not contralateral motor/sensory deficits.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure          | Location             | Primary Function                               | Lesion Symptoms                                                                 | Vascular Supply (Primary) |
|--------------------|----------------------|------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------|
| **Precentral Gyrus** | Frontal Lobe, ant. to central sulcus | Primary Motor Cortex (voluntary movement) | Contralateral hemiparesis/hemiplegia, facial droop, dysarthria, dysphagia          | Middle Cerebral Artery   |
| Postcentral Gyrus   | Parietal Lobe, post. to central sulcus | Primary Somatosensory Cortex (sensation) | Contralateral hemisensory loss (touch, pain, temperature, proprioception)         | Middle Cerebral Artery   |
| Angular Gyrus       | Parietal Lobe        | Association Cortex (language, spatial cognition) | Gerstmann syndrome (agraphia, acalculia, agnosia, disorientation), alexia without agraphia | Middle Cerebral Artery   |
| Temporal Lobe       | Temporal Lobe        | Auditory processing, memory, language comprehension | Auditory hallucinations, memory deficits, Wernicke's aphasia                      | Middle Cerebral Artery   |
| Occipital Lobe      | Occipital Lobe       | Visual processing                               | Visual field defects (hemianopia, quadrantanopia)                                | Posterior Cerebral Artery |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The primary motor cortex is located in the precentral gyrus of the frontal lobe. Damage to this area causes contralateral weakness, particularly affecting the face and upper limb due to the motor homunculus. Strokes affecting the middle cerebral artery commonly involve the precentral and postcentral gyri, leading to contralateral motor and sensory deficits. Differentiating cortical lesions based on symptom localization (motor vs. sensory vs. higher-order functions) is crucial.

<a id='question-030'></a>

### Question 030: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 30 (sulci.)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Identify the specific cortical sulcus based on its location relative to other major sulci and its functional association, particularly in the context of sensory processing. (识别特定皮层裂的解剖位置及其功能关联，特别是感觉处理。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department after experiencing sudden onset of right-sided numbness and tingling affecting his face, arm, and leg. His symptoms began approximately 30 minutes prior to arrival. Neurological examination reveals decreased sensation to light touch and pinprick on the right side of his body, including the face. Motor strength is currently intact, but reflexes are normal bilaterally. A non-contrast head CT scan is unremarkable. An MRI scan performed 24 hours later shows a small, well-defined infarct in the left parietal lobe, specifically involving the region immediately posterior to the central sulcus and superior to the temporal lobe. The lesion appears to be located within a prominent sulcus that separates the postcentral gyrus from the superior parietal lobule.

Which anatomical structure is most likely affected by the infarct described?

(A) Lateral Sulcus (Sylvian Fissure)
(B) Longitudinal Fissure
(C) Central Sulcus (Rolandic Fissure)
(D) Parieto-occipital Sulcus (Hippocampal Sulcus)
(E) Calcarine Sulcus

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with sudden onset right-sided sensory loss (face, arm, leg). This points towards a contralateral (left hemisphere) lesion affecting sensory pathways.
- **Neurological Exam:** Decreased sensation on the right side confirms a left hemisphere lesion. Intact motor strength suggests the primary motor cortex is spared, or the infarct is primarily sensory.
- **Imaging:** MRI shows an infarct in the left parietal lobe, posterior to the central sulcus and superior to the temporal lobe. The lesion is within a prominent sulcus separating the postcentral gyrus from the superior parietal lobule.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the anatomical structure affected by the infarct.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Location:** The infarct is in the left parietal lobe, posterior to the central sulcus (which separates the frontal and parietal lobes, containing the primary motor and somatosensory cortices, respectively) and superior to the temporal lobe (separated by the lateral sulcus).
- **Specific Sulcus:** The description places the lesion within a sulcus separating the postcentral gyrus (primary somatosensory cortex) from the superior parietal lobule. This specific sulcus is the **Parieto-occipital Sulcus**, also known as the **Hippocampal Sulcus**. It runs from the lateral surface towards the medial surface, separating the parietal lobe from the occipital lobe.
- **Function:** The parietal lobe, particularly the postcentral gyrus and superior parietal lobule, is crucial for processing somatosensory information (touch, temperature, pain, proprioception). An infarct in this region, especially within the parieto-occipital sulcus, would explain the contralateral sensory deficits described.
- **Differential Sulci:**
    - **Lateral Sulcus (Sylvian Fissure):** Separates the temporal lobe from the frontal and parietal lobes. Lesions here often affect auditory processing (temporal lobe) or language (dominant hemisphere, frontal/parietal).
    - **Longitudinal Fissure:** Separates the left and right cerebral hemispheres. Lesions are rare and typically involve bilateral structures.
    - **Central Sulcus (Rolandic Fissure):** Separates the frontal lobe (precentral gyrus - motor cortex) from the parietal lobe (postcentral gyrus - somatosensory cortex). A lesion *within* the central sulcus itself is less common; lesions affecting the adjacent gyri cause motor or sensory deficits. The vignette specifies the lesion is *posterior* to the central sulcus.
    - **Calcarine Sulcus:** Located on the medial surface of the occipital lobe, containing the primary visual cortex. Lesions cause visual field defects.

##### 3. Embryological Origin (胚胎发生)
- The cerebral cortex develops from the neural tube via neurogenesis and cortical folding. The sulci represent infoldings of the cortical surface, increasing surface area. The specific pattern of sulci is genetically determined but can be influenced by developmental factors. The parieto-occipital sulcus forms as the parietal and occipital lobes differentiate.

##### 4. Vascular Supply (血管供应)
- The parietal lobe is primarily supplied by branches of the middle cerebral artery (MCA), particularly the parieto-occipital branch. An occlusion or stenosis of this branch could lead to an infarct in the described location.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Lateral Sulcus (Sylvian Fissure):** This sulcus separates the temporal lobe from the frontal and parietal lobes. While infarcts can occur here, they typically affect functions associated with the temporal lobe (hearing, memory) or language areas (if in the dominant hemisphere, involving frontal/parietal operculum). The described location (posterior to central sulcus, superior to temporal lobe) and sensory deficits are inconsistent with a primary Lateral Sulcus lesion.
- (B) **Longitudinal Fissure:** This fissure divides the two cerebral hemispheres. Lesions affecting this structure are extremely rare and would likely involve structures spanning both hemispheres, not a focal infarct causing unilateral sensory loss.
- (C) **Central Sulcus (Rolandic Fissure):** This sulcus separates the motor cortex (precentral gyrus) from the somatosensory cortex (postcentral gyrus). While lesions *adjacent* to this sulcus cause motor or sensory deficits, the vignette explicitly states the infarct is *posterior* to the central sulcus, within the parietal lobe. A lesion solely within the central sulcus is less likely to cause the described pattern of sensory loss without motor involvement.
- (E) **Calcarine Sulcus:** This sulcus is located on the medial surface of the occipital lobe and houses the primary visual cortex. Lesions here cause visual deficits (hemianopia, quadrantanopia), not sensory loss in the limbs and face.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                 | Location                                                                 | Separates                                                                 | Primary Function Affected by Lesion                               | Clinical Presentation                                                                 |
| :------------------------ | :----------------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------ | :--------------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| **Parieto-occipital Sulcus** | Lateral surface, posterior parietal lobe, separating parietal from occipital | Parietal lobe (postcentral gyrus) from Occipital lobe                      | Somatosensory processing (parietal), Visual processing (occipital)     | Contralateral sensory loss (face, arm, leg), possible visual field defects (posterior) |
| Lateral Sulcus            | Lateral surface, separating temporal from frontal/parietal lobes           | Temporal lobe from Frontal & Parietal lobes                               | Auditory processing, Language (dominant hemisphere), Memory             | Hearing loss, Aphasia, Memory impairment                                               |
| Longitudinal Fissure      | Divides cerebral hemispheres                                              | Left hemisphere from Right hemisphere                                      | Bilateral structures (rarely affected focally)                          | Rare, bilateral deficits                                                              |
| Central Sulcus            | Lateral surface, separating frontal from parietal lobes                    | Frontal lobe (precentral gyrus) from Parietal lobe (postcentral gyrus) | Motor control (frontal), Somatosensory processing (parietal)           | Contralateral motor weakness/sensory loss (depending on affected gyrus)                |
| Calcarine Sulcus          | Medial surface, occipital lobe                                             | Occipital lobe                                                             | Primary visual cortex                                                   | Visual field defects (hemianopia, quadrantanopia)                                      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The parieto-occipital sulcus is a key landmark separating the parietal and occipital lobes on the lateral surface of the brain. Lesions in this area, particularly involving the superior parietal lobule or the postcentral gyrus adjacent to it, typically cause contralateral sensory deficits. Differentiating this sulcus from the central, lateral, and calcarine sulci is crucial based on its specific location and the resulting neurological deficits. Remember the central sulcus separates motor and sensory cortex, the lateral sulcus separates temporal from frontal/parietal, and the calcarine sulcus is on the medial occipital lobe for vision.

<a id='question-031'></a>

### Question 031: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 31 (cerebrum)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the functional localization within the cerebral cortex, specifically the motor cortex (primary motor cortex, premotor cortex, supplementary motor area) and its relationship to specific body parts, as well as the vascular supply (middle cerebral artery) and its potential occlusion leading to specific neurological deficits. (理解大脑皮层的功能定位，特别是运动皮层（初级运动皮层、前运动皮层、辅助运动区）与特定身体部位的关系，以及其血管供应（大脑中动脉）及其潜在闭塞导致特定的神经功能缺损。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old right-handed male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with acute onset of right-sided weakness and numbness. The symptoms began approximately 2 hours ago while he was watching television. On examination, his vital signs are stable. Neurological examination reveals significant weakness in the right face, arm, and leg (strength 2/5 throughout the right side). Sensory examination shows decreased sensation to light touch and pinprick on the right side of his body. He is unable to speak fluently, exhibiting non-fluent aphasia (Broca's aphasia), and has difficulty understanding complex commands. His right eye deviates slightly downward and inward (right gaze preference). Reflexes are brisk on the right side. An emergent non-contrast head CT scan shows no evidence of hemorrhage. MRI confirms an acute ischemic stroke affecting the left cerebral hemisphere. Which area of the left cerebral cortex is most likely affected, leading to this constellation of symptoms?

(A) Primary Somatosensory Cortex (Postcentral Gyrus)
(B) Primary Motor Cortex (Precentral Gyrus)
(C) Premotor Cortex
(D) Broca's Area
(E) Wernicke's Area

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia). This increases the likelihood of a stroke.
*   **Onset:** Acute onset (2 hours ago) suggests a vascular event (stroke).
*   **Symptoms:** Right-sided weakness (face, arm, leg) and numbness indicate a lesion in the contralateral (left) hemisphere affecting motor and sensory pathways.
*   **Speech:** Non-fluent aphasia (Broca's aphasia) points to damage in the dominant (usually left) hemisphere's frontal lobe, specifically the area responsible for speech production.
*   **Comprehension:** Difficulty understanding complex commands suggests involvement of language processing areas.
*   **Eye Deviation:** Right gaze preference (eyes looking towards the side of the lesion) is characteristic of a lesion affecting the left frontal lobe, specifically the frontal eye fields.
*   **Imaging:** MRI confirms acute ischemic stroke in the left hemisphere.
*   **Question:** Asks for the *most likely* affected area of the *left cerebral cortex* causing this specific combination of motor, sensory, and language deficits.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
*   **Motor Cortex (Precentral Gyrus):** Located in the frontal lobe, just anterior to the central sulcus. It controls voluntary movements of the contralateral body. The homunculus representation shows the face, arm, and hand having large cortical areas, consistent with the patient's significant weakness in these regions. A lesion here would cause contralateral hemiparesis/hemiplegia.
*   **Primary Somatosensory Cortex (Postcentral Gyrus):** Located posterior to the central sulcus. Receives sensory information from the contralateral body. A lesion here would cause contralateral sensory loss.
*   **Premotor Cortex:** Located anterior to the primary motor cortex. Involved in planning and sequencing movements. Lesions typically cause apraxia (difficulty performing learned movements) rather than primary weakness, although weakness can occur if the lesion extends into the primary motor cortex.
*   **Broca's Area:** Located in the inferior frontal gyrus of the dominant hemisphere (usually left). Responsible for speech production. Damage causes expressive (non-fluent) aphasia.
*   **Wernicke's Area:** Located in the posterior superior temporal gyrus of the dominant hemisphere. Responsible for language comprehension. Damage causes receptive (fluent) aphasia.
*   **Frontal Eye Fields:** Located in the frontal lobe, anterior to Broca's area. Control voluntary eye movements. Lesions cause deviation of the eyes towards the side of the lesion (contralateral gaze preference).
*   **Vascular Supply:** The Middle Cerebral Artery (MCA) supplies the lateral surface of the cerebral hemisphere, including the motor cortex, sensory cortex, Broca's area, Wernicke's area, and frontal eye fields. An MCA occlusion is a common cause of stroke presenting with contralateral hemiparesis, sensory loss, aphasia (if dominant hemisphere), and gaze preference.
*   **Synthesis:** The patient presents with right hemiparesis (face, arm, leg), right sensory loss, Broca's aphasia, and right gaze preference, all consistent with a lesion in the left hemisphere affecting the motor cortex, sensory cortex, Broca's area, and frontal eye fields. The primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus) is the most central structure responsible for the profound contralateral motor weakness affecting the face, arm, and leg, which is a hallmark of this presentation. While Broca's area is also involved (explaining the aphasia), the question asks for the area most likely affected leading to *this constellation* of symptoms, and the motor cortex is the primary driver of the significant motor deficit described. The MCA territory encompasses all these areas, making MCA occlusion the likely cause.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Primary Somatosensory Cortex (Postcentral Gyrus):** While a lesion here would cause contralateral sensory loss (present in the patient), it does not directly explain the profound motor weakness, non-fluent aphasia, or gaze preference. The motor symptoms are more prominent and point towards the motor cortex.
- (C) **Premotor Cortex:** Lesions here primarily cause apraxia and difficulties with motor planning, often less severe weakness than a primary motor cortex lesion. While it's adjacent to the primary motor cortex and often affected together in MCA strokes, it's not the primary area responsible for the *initial* and *most significant* motor deficit described (strength 2/5).
- (D) **Broca's Area:** A lesion here explains the non-fluent aphasia, but not the significant motor weakness, sensory loss, or gaze preference. It is part of the affected territory but not the primary cause of the motor symptoms.
- (E) **Wernicke's Area:** A lesion here causes receptive (fluent) aphasia, which is the opposite of what the patient exhibits (non-fluent aphasia). Therefore, this area is unlikely to be the primary site of the lesion causing these symptoms.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature                 | Primary Motor Cortex (Precentral Gyrus) | Primary Somatosensory Cortex (Postcentral Gyrus) | Premotor Cortex | Broca's Area | Wernicke's Area |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- | :------------- | :----------- | :-------------- |
| **Location**            | Frontal lobe, anterior to central sulcus | Parietal lobe, posterior to central sulcus      | Frontal lobe, anterior to motor cortex | Frontal lobe, inferior frontal gyrus (dominant) | Temporal lobe, posterior superior temporal gyrus (dominant) |
| **Primary Function**    | Voluntary motor control                  | Sensory perception                             | Motor planning | Speech production | Language comprehension |
| **Lesion Effect**       | Contralateral hemiparesis/hemiplegia    | Contralateral sensory loss                     | Apraxia, motor planning deficits | Expressive (non-fluent) aphasia | Receptive (fluent) aphasia |
| **Patient Symptoms**    | Right hemiparesis (face, arm, leg)      | Right sensory loss                             | Less prominent weakness, apraxia | Non-fluent aphasia | Fluent aphasia |
| **Gaze Preference**     | Possible (if frontal eye fields involved) | No                                             | No             | No           | No              |
| **Key Association**     | Homunculus representation              | Homunculus representation                      | Motor planning | Speech output | Language input |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The clinical presentation of contralateral hemiparesis, sensory loss, aphasia, and gaze preference strongly suggests a stroke affecting the lateral surface of the cerebral hemisphere, typically supplied by the Middle Cerebral Artery (MCA). The primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus) is the key area responsible for the profound motor weakness affecting the face, arm, and leg, making it the most likely primary site of the lesion described. Differentiating between motor, sensory, language, and premotor cortex functions and their corresponding deficits is crucial for localizing neurological lesions. Remember Broca's area causes expressive aphasia, Wernicke's area causes receptive aphasia, and the frontal eye fields control conjugate gaze.
