<a id='question-032'></a>

### Question 032: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 32 (divided)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the functional localization within the cerebral cortex, specifically the relationship between the primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus) and the internal capsule, and how lesions in these areas manifest clinically. (理解大脑皮层的功能定位，特别是初级运动皮层（中央前回）与内囊之间的关系，以及这些区域的病变如何表现出来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with acute onset of right-sided weakness and numbness. The symptoms began approximately 2 hours ago while he was watching television. On examination, his vital signs are stable. Neurological examination reveals significant weakness in the right upper and lower extremities, with facial droop on the right side. Sensory examination shows decreased sensation to light touch and pinprick on the right side of his body, predominantly affecting the face, arm, and leg. Reflexes are hyperactive on the right side, and a positive Babinski sign is present on the right. MRI of the brain reveals an acute ischemic infarct in the left hemisphere. The lesion is located deep within the white matter, specifically involving the posterior limb of the internal capsule.

Which of the following structures is MOST likely affected by this infarct, leading to the patient's clinical presentation?

(A) Broca's area
(B) Primary somatosensory cortex
(C) Primary motor cortex
(D) Wernicke's area
(E) Visual cortex

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male, history of hypertension/hyperlipidemia (risk factors for stroke), acute onset right-sided weakness and numbness. This points towards a vascular event (stroke) affecting the contralateral (left) side of the brain.
- **Neurological Examination:** Right-sided weakness (hemiparesis) and numbness (hemianesthesia) affecting the face, arm, and leg. This indicates involvement of the corticospinal tract (motor) and spinothalamic tract (sensory) pathways. Hyperreflexia and positive Babinski sign on the right side further confirm an upper motor neuron lesion on the left side.
- **Imaging Findings:** Acute ischemic infarct in the left hemisphere, specifically in the posterior limb of the internal capsule. The internal capsule is a critical white matter structure containing major ascending and descending tracts.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which structure is MOST likely affected, given the location of the infarct (left posterior limb of internal capsule) and the resulting contralateral (right-sided) motor and sensory deficits.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Internal Capsule:** A thick band of white matter located deep within the cerebral hemispheres, between the thalamus and the basal ganglia. It is a crucial pathway for ascending sensory fibers and descending motor fibers.
- **Posterior Limb of Internal Capsule:** Contains the corticospinal tract (motor control of contralateral limbs and face), corticobulbar tract (motor control of contralateral face, head, and neck muscles), thalamocortical radiations (sensory information from thalamus to somatosensory cortex), and optic radiations (visual information from lateral geniculate nucleus to visual cortex).
- **Corticospinal Tract:** Originates primarily from the primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus, Brodmann area 4) in the frontal lobe. Descends through the posterior limb of the internal capsule, then through the brainstem (decussates in the medulla), and finally synapses on lower motor neurons in the spinal cord. Damage here causes contralateral hemiparesis/hemiplegia.
- **Thalamocortical Radiations:** Carry sensory information (pain, temperature, touch, proprioception) from the thalamus to the primary somatosensory cortex (postcentral gyrus, Brodmann areas 3, 1, 2) in the parietal lobe. Damage here causes contralateral hemisensory loss.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's right-sided hemiparesis and hemisensory loss are classic signs of a lesion affecting the corticospinal tract and thalamocortical radiations within the left posterior limb of the internal capsule. These tracts originate from or project to the primary motor cortex and primary somatosensory cortex, respectively. Since the infarct is deep within the internal capsule, it directly affects these tracts *before* they reach their cortical destinations. However, the question asks which *structure* is most likely affected, implying the origin or destination of these tracts. The primary motor cortex is the origin of the corticospinal tract, which is severely affected. The primary somatosensory cortex is the destination of the thalamocortical radiations, also affected. Given the prominent motor deficits (weakness, hyperreflexia, Babinski), the primary motor cortex is a key structure functionally impacted by the lesion in the internal capsule.
- **Option Analysis:**
    - (A) Broca's area (inferior frontal gyrus, usually left hemisphere): Involved in speech production. Lesions cause expressive aphasia. Not directly affected by a posterior limb internal capsule infarct causing contralateral motor/sensory deficits.
    - (B) Primary somatosensory cortex (postcentral gyrus, parietal lobe): Receives sensory input via thalamocortical radiations. Lesions cause contralateral sensory loss. While affected by the infarct (due to damage to thalamocortical radiations), the motor deficits point strongly to involvement of the motor cortex as well.
    - (C) Primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus, frontal lobe): Origin of the corticospinal tract. Lesions cause contralateral weakness/paralysis. This is the origin of the major tract affected by the infarct in the internal capsule, explaining the motor symptoms.
    - (D) Wernicke's area (superior temporal gyrus, usually left hemisphere): Involved in language comprehension. Lesions cause receptive aphasia. Not directly affected by a posterior limb internal capsule infarct causing contralateral motor/sensory deficits.
    - (E) Visual cortex (occipital lobe): Receives visual input via optic radiations. Lesions cause visual field defects. While optic radiations pass through the internal capsule (specifically the retrolenticular part, which is posterior to the posterior limb), the primary symptoms described (motor and sensory deficits of the face, arm, leg) are not typical of a visual cortex lesion.

- **Conclusion:** The infarct in the left posterior limb of the internal capsule disrupts the corticospinal tract (originating from the primary motor cortex) and thalamocortical radiations (projecting to the primary somatosensory cortex), leading to right-sided motor and sensory deficits. The primary motor cortex is the origin of the affected motor pathway.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Broca's area: Incorrect. Located in the inferior frontal gyrus. Lesions cause expressive aphasia. The infarct location (posterior limb of internal capsule) and symptoms (contralateral motor/sensory deficits) do not correlate with Broca's area function. Test-takers might confuse frontal lobe structures, but the specific symptoms and location rule this out.
- (B) Primary somatosensory cortex: Incorrect. Located in the postcentral gyrus of the parietal lobe. While thalamocortical radiations projecting *to* this area pass through the posterior limb of the internal capsule and are affected by the infarct, causing sensory loss, the primary motor cortex (origin of the corticospinal tract) is also significantly affected, explaining the prominent motor deficits. The question asks for the *most likely* structure affected, and the motor cortex is the origin of the primary affected pathway causing the most prominent symptoms (weakness).
- (D) Wernicke's area: Incorrect. Located in the superior temporal gyrus. Lesions cause receptive aphasia. This area is not directly related to the posterior limb of the internal capsule or the observed motor/sensory deficits. Test-takers might confuse language areas in the frontal/temporal lobes.
- (E) Visual cortex: Incorrect. Located in the occipital lobe. Lesions cause visual field defects. Optic radiations pass through the internal capsule, but typically the retrolenticular part, and the symptoms described are motor and sensory, not visual. Test-takers might confuse different white matter tracts.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                 | Location                      | Function                                     | Lesion Symptoms                                  | Relation to Internal Capsule |
| :------------------------ | :---------------------------- | :------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------- |
| Primary Motor Cortex      | Precentral Gyrus (Frontal Lobe) | Voluntary motor control                       | Contralateral weakness/paralysis                 | Origin of corticospinal tract |
| Primary Somatosensory Cortex | Postcentral Gyrus (Parietal Lobe) | Processing touch, pain, temperature, proprioception | Contralateral sensory loss                       | Destination of thalamocortical radiations |
| Broca's Area             | Inferior Frontal Gyrus        | Speech production                             | Expressive aphasia                               | Not directly related        |
| Wernicke's Area          | Superior Temporal Gyrus       | Language comprehension                        | Receptive aphasia                                | Not directly related        |
| Visual Cortex             | Occipital Lobe                | Visual processing                             | Visual field defects                             | Optic radiations pass nearby |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The posterior limb of the internal capsule contains critical ascending (sensory) and descending (motor) pathways. An infarct here typically causes contralateral hemiparesis (due to corticospinal tract damage) and hemisensory loss (due to thalamocortical radiation damage). The primary motor cortex is the origin of the corticospinal tract, and the primary somatosensory cortex is the destination of the thalamocortical radiations, both functionally affected by a lesion in the internal capsule. Key differentials include lesions in the motor cortex itself (more localized weakness, potentially affecting face/arm more than leg), sensory cortex (isolated sensory loss), or other parts of the internal capsule (e.g., retrolenticular part affecting optic radiations).

<a id='question-033'></a>

### Question 033: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 33 (according)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the functional localization within the cerebral cortex, specifically the relationship between the precentral gyrus (primary motor cortex) and the internal capsule, and the consequences of vascular lesions affecting these structures. (理解大脑皮层的功能定位，特别是前中回（初级运动皮层）与内囊的关系，以及血管病变对这些结构的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with acute onset of right-sided weakness and numbness. The symptoms began approximately 2 hours prior to arrival. Neurological examination reveals significant weakness in the right face, arm, and leg (strength 2/5), with associated sensory loss to light touch and pinprick on the right side of the body. The patient is alert and oriented, with no speech deficits or visual field cuts. Cranial nerve examination is normal except for mild right facial droop. Reflexes are hyperactive on the right side. An emergent non-contrast head CT scan shows a small, non-enhancing hypodensity in the left internal capsule region.

Which of the following structures is most likely affected by the lesion described?

(A) Left superior temporal gyrus
(B) Left inferior parietal lobule
(C) Left precentral gyrus
(D) Left postcentral gyrus
(E) Left supplementary motor area

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 68-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia) presenting with acute onset right-sided weakness and numbness. This strongly suggests a stroke (cerebrovascular accident, CVA).
- **Neurological Examination:** Right-sided weakness (hemiparesis) affecting the face, arm, and leg (contralateral to the lesion) indicates involvement of the motor pathways controlling these regions. Right-sided sensory loss (hemianesthesia) indicates involvement of the sensory pathways. The pattern (face, arm, leg) suggests involvement of the motor and sensory cortex or descending/ascending tracts. Hyperactive reflexes on the right side are consistent with an upper motor neuron lesion. Absence of speech deficits (aphasia) or visual field cuts (hemianopia) helps localize the lesion away from Broca's/Wernicke's areas and the optic radiations, respectively.
- **Imaging:** Non-contrast head CT shows a hypodensity in the left internal capsule. This confirms an ischemic stroke in that location.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which *structure* is most likely affected. The lesion is in the internal capsule, but the question asks which *cortical* structure's fibers are primarily affected *within* the internal capsule, leading to the observed contralateral motor and sensory deficits.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Internal Capsule:** The internal capsule is a white matter structure situated between the thalamus and the basal ganglia (caudate nucleus and lentiform nucleus). It contains ascending sensory fibers (thalamocortical radiations) and descending motor fibers (corticospinal, corticobulbar, and corticopontine tracts).
- **Motor Fibers:** The corticospinal tract originates primarily from the primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus, Brodmann area 4) and the premotor cortex (premotor cortex, Brodmann area 6) and supplementary motor area (SMA, Brodmann area 8). These fibers descend through the internal capsule, forming the posterior limb. A lesion in the posterior limb of the internal capsule affects the corticospinal tract, causing contralateral hemiparesis (weakness of face, arm, and leg).
- **Sensory Fibers:** The thalamocortical radiations carry sensory information from the thalamus to the primary somatosensory cortex (postcentral gyrus, Brodmann areas 3, 1, 2). These fibers pass through the posterior limb of the internal capsule, posterior to the corticospinal tract. A lesion in the posterior limb of the internal capsule affects these radiations, causing contralateral hemianesthesia (loss of sensation).
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's symptoms (right hemiparesis and hemianesthesia) are classic signs of a lesion affecting the corticospinal tract and thalamocortical radiations in the posterior limb of the left internal capsule. These fibers originate from the left precentral gyrus (motor) and terminate in the left postcentral gyrus (sensory). However, the question asks which structure is *affected* by the lesion *within* the internal capsule. The motor fibers passing through the internal capsule originate from the precentral gyrus. Therefore, the precentral gyrus is the cortical area whose output fibers are interrupted by the lesion in the internal capsule, leading to the motor deficits. The sensory deficits are due to interruption of fibers originating from the postcentral gyrus. Since the question asks for *a* structure affected, and the motor deficits are prominent and involve the face, arm, and leg, the precentral gyrus is the most direct cortical source of the affected motor fibers.
- **Why other options are incorrect:**
    - (A) Superior temporal gyrus: Primarily involved in auditory processing (Wernicke's area in the dominant hemisphere). Lesions cause auditory agnosia or receptive aphasia. Not consistent with motor/sensory deficits.
    - (B) Inferior parietal lobule: Involved in sensory integration, spatial awareness, and language (angular gyrus, supramarginal gyrus). Lesions can cause contralateral sensory neglect, astereognosis, or conduction aphasia. Not consistent with the primary motor/sensory deficits described.
    - (D) Postcentral gyrus: Primary somatosensory cortex. Lesions cause contralateral sensory loss. While sensory fibers pass through the internal capsule, the question stem emphasizes the motor deficits (hemiparesis) as well, and the precentral gyrus is the origin of the major motor fibers affected. Often, internal capsule strokes affect both motor and sensory fibers. However, the precentral gyrus is the origin of the *motor* fibers causing the hemiparesis, making it a key structure affected functionally.
    - (E) Supplementary motor area: Involved in motor planning and sequencing. Lesions typically cause apraxia or difficulty with complex motor tasks, not isolated hemiparesis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Left superior temporal gyrus: Incorrect. This area is primarily auditory and language-related (Wernicke's area). A lesion here would cause auditory processing deficits or receptive aphasia, not contralateral motor and sensory loss. Test-takers might confuse it with other temporal lobe structures involved in language.
- (B) Left inferior parietal lobule: Incorrect. This area is involved in somatosensory integration, spatial processing, and aspects of language. Lesions can cause contralateral sensory neglect, astereognosis, or Gerstmann syndrome. It does not directly give rise to the major motor fibers affected in this case. Test-takers might confuse it with sensory processing areas.
- (D) Left postcentral gyrus: Incorrect. This is the primary somatosensory cortex. While sensory fibers from the thalamus pass through the internal capsule and terminate here, the prominent motor deficits (hemiparesis) point to the interruption of motor fibers originating from the precentral gyrus. The lesion affects both motor and sensory pathways, but the precentral gyrus is the origin of the motor fibers. Test-takers might focus solely on the sensory loss and choose this option.
- (E) Left supplementary motor area: Incorrect. This area is involved in motor planning and sequencing. Lesions typically cause apraxia or difficulty initiating complex movements, not the widespread hemiparesis seen here. Test-takers might confuse it with other motor cortex areas.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                 | Location                                  | Primary Function                                  | Lesion Effects                                                                 | Relation to Internal Capsule |
| :------------------------ | :---------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ | :----------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------- |
| Precentral Gyrus (C)      | Anterior to Central Sulcus                | Primary Motor Cortex (execution of voluntary movement) | Contralateral hemiparesis/hemiplegia (face, arm, leg)                           | Origin of motor fibers in IC |
| Postcentral Gyrus (D)     | Posterior to Central Sulcus               | Primary Somatosensory Cortex (processing touch, pain, temp) | Contralateral hemianesthesia/sensory loss                                      | Termination of sensory fibers in IC |
| Superior Temporal Gyrus (A) | Superior aspect of Temporal Lobe          | Auditory processing, language comprehension (Wernicke's) | Auditory agnosia, receptive aphasia                                            | Not directly related to IC   |
| Inferior Parietal Lobule (B) | Inferior aspect of Parietal Lobe          | Sensory integration, spatial awareness, language (angular/supramarginal gyri) | Contralateral sensory neglect, astereognosis, conduction aphasia               | Not directly related to IC   |
| Supplementary Motor Area (E) | Medial surface, anterior to Precentral Gyrus | Motor planning, sequencing                          | Apraxia, difficulty with complex motor sequences, impaired bimanual coordination | Origin of motor fibers in IC |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The internal capsule contains critical ascending sensory and descending motor pathways. A lesion here, often due to stroke, typically causes contralateral hemiparesis and hemianesthesia. The motor fibers originate from the precentral gyrus, and the sensory fibers terminate in the postcentral gyrus. Understanding the functional anatomy of the cerebral cortex and the pathways within the white matter is crucial for localizing neurological deficits. Key differentials include lesions in the motor cortex itself (precentral gyrus), sensory cortex (postcentral gyrus), or other white matter tracts, but the internal capsule is a common site for combined motor and sensory deficits due to its dense packing of fibers.

<a id='question-034'></a>

### Question 034: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 34 (prominent)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Identify the specific cortical area (Part 1 Focus 34, prominent) responsible for a given function or affected by a specific lesion, understanding its location, blood supply, and functional specialization. (识别特定皮质区域（Part 1 Focus 34，突出区域）负责的特定功能或受特定病变影响，理解其位置、血供和功能专业化。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with acute onset of right-sided weakness and numbness, predominantly affecting his right face and arm, but sparing his leg. He also exhibits expressive aphasia (difficulty speaking) and right facial droop. A non-contrast CT scan of the head reveals a left middle cerebral artery (MCA) territory infarct. Neurological examination confirms right hemiparesis (face and arm > leg), right hemisensory loss (face and arm > leg), right facial droop (lower face), and expressive aphasia. Which cortical area, prominently located within the affected MCA territory, is most likely responsible for the patient's expressive aphasia?

(A) Primary Motor Cortex (Area 4)
(B) Primary Somatosensory Cortex (Area 1, 2, 3)
(C) Broca's Area (Area 44, 45)
(D) Wernicke's Area (Area 22)
(E) Visual Cortex (Area 17)

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 68-year-old male, history of vascular risk factors (HTN, hyperlipidemia), acute onset right hemiparesis/hemisensory loss (face/arm > leg), right facial droop (lower face), expressive aphasia.
- **Imaging:** Left MCA territory infarct.
- **Neurological Exam:** Right hemiparesis (face/arm > leg), right hemisensory loss (face/arm > leg), right lower facial droop, expressive aphasia.
- **Key Clues:**
    - **Left MCA infarct:** This localizes the lesion to the left cerebral hemisphere, specifically the territory supplied by the MCA. The MCA supplies the lateral surface of the frontal, parietal, and temporal lobes.
    - **Right hemiparesis/hemisensory loss (face/arm > leg):** This pattern is characteristic of a lesion affecting the motor and sensory homunculi in the precentral and postcentral gyri, respectively, which are located within the MCA territory. The disproportionate involvement of the face and arm compared to the leg reflects the somatotopic organization of the motor and sensory cortices.
    - **Right lower facial droop:** This indicates an upper motor neuron lesion affecting the corticobulbar tract fibers controlling the contralateral lower face muscles (orbicularis oris, buccinator). The upper face receives bilateral cortical innervation, hence sparing.
    - **Expressive aphasia:** This is a language disorder characterized by difficulty producing speech, despite relatively intact comprehension. It is typically associated with damage to specific language areas in the dominant hemisphere (usually the left).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the cortical area responsible for expressive aphasia within the affected MCA territory.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **MCA Territory:** The MCA supplies a large portion of the lateral cerebral cortex, including the primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus, Area 4), primary somatosensory cortex (postcentral gyrus, Areas 1, 2, 3), premotor cortex (Area 6), supplementary motor area (Area 8), Broca's area (inferior frontal gyrus, Areas 44 and 45), Wernicke's area (superior temporal gyrus, Area 22), and parts of the parietal and temporal lobes.
- **Expressive Aphasia (Broca's Aphasia):** This type of aphasia results from damage to Broca's area, located in the inferior frontal gyrus of the dominant hemisphere (typically the left). Broca's area is crucial for speech production, grammar, and articulation. Damage leads to non-fluent speech, effortful articulation, and often agrammatism.
- **Broca's Area (Areas 44 & 45):** Located in the pars opercularis and pars triangularis of the inferior frontal gyrus, anterior to the primary motor cortex controlling the face and tongue. It is supplied by the MCA.
- **Wernicke's Area (Area 22):** Located in the posterior superior temporal gyrus, crucial for language comprehension. Damage leads to receptive aphasia (fluent but nonsensical speech, impaired comprehension). It is also supplied by the MCA.
- **Primary Motor Cortex (Area 4):** Located in the precentral gyrus, responsible for voluntary motor control. Damage causes contralateral weakness/paralysis.
- **Primary Somatosensory Cortex (Areas 1, 2, 3):** Located in the postcentral gyrus, responsible for processing sensory information (touch, pain, temperature, proprioception). Damage causes contralateral sensory loss.
- **Visual Cortex (Area 17):** Located in the occipital lobe, responsible for processing visual information. Damage causes visual field defects.
- **Correlation:** The patient's symptoms (right hemiparesis/hemisensory loss, right lower facial droop, expressive aphasia) and the location of the infarct (left MCA territory) strongly point to damage involving the motor/sensory cortex and Broca's area. The specific symptom of expressive aphasia directly implicates Broca's area.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Primary Motor Cortex (Area 4):** Located in the precentral gyrus, part of the MCA territory. Damage causes contralateral weakness (hemiparesis), which the patient has. However, it is not primarily responsible for expressive aphasia. While adjacent to Broca's area, the motor cortex's main function is motor control. Test-takers might confuse it due to its location within the MCA territory and the presence of hemiparesis.
- (B) **Primary Somatosensory Cortex (Area 1, 2, 3):** Located in the postcentral gyrus, part of the MCA territory. Damage causes contralateral sensory loss, which the patient has. However, it is not responsible for expressive aphasia. Test-takers might confuse it due to its location within the MCA territory and the presence of hemisensory loss.
- (D) **Wernicke's Area (Area 22):** Located in the superior temporal gyrus, part of the MCA territory. Damage causes receptive aphasia (difficulty understanding language), not expressive aphasia (difficulty producing language). The patient has expressive aphasia. Test-takers might confuse it with Broca's area as both are language centers within the MCA territory, but they have distinct functions.
- (E) **Visual Cortex (Area 17):** Located in the occipital lobe, primarily supplied by the posterior cerebral artery (PCA), although the most anterior part can receive supply from the MCA. Damage causes visual deficits. The patient's symptoms are motor and language-related, not visual. This option is incorrect based on both location (primarily PCA territory) and function.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure             | Location                                  | Blood Supply | Primary Function                               | Clinical Deficit (Lesion)                               |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------------|--------------|------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------|
| Broca's Area (44,45) | Inferior Frontal Gyrus (Dominant Hem.)   | MCA          | Speech Production, Grammar, Articulation        | Expressive (Broca's) Aphasia                            |
| Wernicke's Area (22) | Superior Temporal Gyrus (Dominant Hem.)   | MCA          | Language Comprehension                          | Receptive (Wernicke's) Aphasia                          |
| Motor Cortex (Area 4) | Precentral Gyrus                           | MCA          | Voluntary Motor Control                          | Contralateral Hemiparesis/Paralysis                    |
| Sensory Cortex (1-3) | Postcentral Gyrus                          | MCA          | Sensory Processing (Touch, Pain, Temp, Proprio.) | Contralateral Hemisensory Loss                         |
| Visual Cortex (Area 17) | Occipital Lobe                             | PCA (Anterior MCA) | Visual Processing                               | Visual Field Defects (e.g., Homonymous Hemianopia)      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The clinical presentation of expressive aphasia following a left MCA stroke strongly localizes the lesion to Broca's area (Areas 44 and 45) in the inferior frontal gyrus. Differentiating Broca's area (expressive aphasia) from Wernicke's area (receptive aphasia), motor cortex (motor deficits), and sensory cortex (sensory deficits) is crucial for localizing lesions within the MCA territory. Remember that the dominant hemisphere (usually left) is responsible for language function in most individuals.

<a id='question-035'></a>

### Question 035: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 35 (fairly)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the functional localization within the cerebral cortex, specifically the precentral gyrus (primary motor cortex) and its relationship to the internal capsule, and the consequences of vascular occlusion affecting this region. (理解大脑皮层的功能定位，特别是中央前回（初级运动皮层）与其与内囊的关系，以及影响该区域血管闭塞的后果。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with acute onset of right-sided weakness and numbness. The symptoms began approximately 2 hours prior to arrival. On examination, his vital signs are stable. Neurological examination reveals significant weakness in the right upper and lower extremities, affecting facial muscles as well, with a central facial palsy (lower face more affected than upper face). Sensory examination shows decreased sensation to light touch and pinprick on the right side of the body, also involving the face. Reflexes are hyperactive on the right side, and a positive Babinski sign is present on the right. A non-contrast head CT scan shows no evidence of hemorrhage. An MRI scan performed 4 hours later reveals an acute ischemic infarct in the left middle cerebral artery (MCA) territory. The infarct primarily involves the lateral aspect of the left frontal and parietal lobes, including the precentral gyrus.

Which of the following structures is most likely affected by the infarct, leading to the patient's motor deficits?

(A) Internal capsule (posterior limb)
(B) Basal ganglia (putamen)
(C) Thalamus (ventral posterior nucleus)
(D) Cerebellum (inferior hemisphere)
(E) Brainstem (pons)

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia) presenting with acute onset right-sided hemiparesis and hemisensory loss. This strongly suggests a stroke.
- **Neurological Examination:** Right-sided weakness (hemiparesis) and numbness (hemianesthesia) involving the face, upper limb, and lower limb indicate a lesion affecting the contralateral (left) motor and sensory pathways. The central facial palsy (lower face > upper face) points to an upper motor neuron lesion. Hyperreflexia and positive Babinski sign on the right further confirm an upper motor neuron lesion.
- **Imaging Findings:** MRI confirms an acute ischemic infarct in the left MCA territory, specifically involving the lateral aspect of the left frontal and parietal lobes, including the precentral gyrus.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the structure most likely affected by the infarct leading to the motor deficits. The motor deficits (right hemiparesis) are contralateral to the infarct (left hemisphere). The infarct involves the precentral gyrus (primary motor cortex).

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Precentral Gyrus:** Located on the posterior aspect of the frontal lobe, the precentral gyrus contains the primary motor cortex (Brodmann area 4). It controls voluntary movements of the contralateral side of the body. The motor homunculus represents the cortical area dedicated to different body parts.
- **Motor Pathways:** Motor commands originate in the motor cortex (precentral gyrus), travel through the corona radiata, and descend through the internal capsule. The internal capsule is a white matter structure containing ascending sensory fibers and descending motor fibers. The posterior limb of the internal capsule contains the corticospinal tract (responsible for voluntary motor control of the limbs and trunk) and the corticobulbar tract (responsible for voluntary motor control of the face, head, and neck).
- **MCA Stroke:** The MCA supplies blood to the lateral surface of the cerebral hemisphere, including the frontal, parietal, and temporal lobes. An infarct in the MCA territory affecting the precentral gyrus will cause contralateral motor deficits.
- **Internal Capsule Involvement:** The corticospinal and corticobulbar tracts, originating from the motor cortex (precentral gyrus), pass through the posterior limb of the internal capsule on their way to the brainstem. A stroke affecting the precentral gyrus itself can cause motor deficits, but often the descending motor fibers within the internal capsule are also affected, especially if the infarct extends deeper or involves the adjacent white matter (corona radiata). Damage to the posterior limb of the internal capsule results in contralateral hemiparesis affecting the face, arm, and leg. Given the location of the infarct (precentral gyrus within the MCA territory) and the resulting contralateral motor deficits (right hemiparesis), the descending motor fibers passing through the adjacent internal capsule are highly likely to be involved. The question asks which structure is *most likely affected* leading to the motor deficits. While the precentral gyrus is the origin, the internal capsule is the critical pathway where damage causes widespread contralateral motor loss. The clinical picture of contralateral hemiparesis involving face, arm, and leg is classic for internal capsule involvement.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Internal capsule (posterior limb)**: This is the correct answer. The corticospinal and corticobulbar tracts, originating from the motor cortex (precentral gyrus), pass through the posterior limb of the internal capsule. An MCA stroke involving the precentral gyrus often extends to or directly affects the adjacent internal capsule, causing contralateral hemiparesis. This matches the patient's presentation.
- (B): **Basal ganglia (putamen)**: The putamen is part of the basal ganglia, involved in motor control, but primarily in initiating and regulating movement, rather than direct execution. Lesions typically cause hypokinetic (e.g., Parkinsonism) or hyperkinetic (e.g., Huntington's) movement disorders, or contralateral hemiballismus. While the MCA supplies the basal ganglia, a lesion primarily affecting the putamen would not typically cause the isolated contralateral hemiparesis and hemisensory loss seen here.
- (C): **Thalamus (ventral posterior nucleus)**: The ventral posterior nucleus (VPL) of the thalamus receives sensory information from the body and relays it to the somatosensory cortex in the parietal lobe. Lesions here cause contralateral sensory loss (hemianesthesia). While the patient has sensory loss, the primary motor deficit (hemiparesis) is not directly explained by a thalamic lesion. Thalamic infarcts are often lacunar strokes affecting smaller branches of the MCA or PCA, and while they cause sensory loss, they don't typically cause significant motor weakness unless they extend to involve nearby structures like the internal capsule. The infarct described involves the cortex and extends laterally, making thalamic involvement less likely as the primary cause of the motor deficit.
- (D): **Cerebellum (inferior hemisphere)**: The cerebellum is primarily involved in coordination, balance, and motor learning. Lesions typically cause ataxia, dysmetria, intention tremor, and nystagmus (ipsilateral cerebellar signs). While the cerebellum receives input from the cerebral cortex and projects to the thalamus, a cerebellar infarct would not cause contralateral hemiparesis and hemisensory loss. The inferior cerebellar artery (often arising from the vertebral or basilar artery) supplies the inferior hemisphere.
- (E): **Brainstem (pons)**: The pons contains descending motor tracts (including corticospinal tracts) and ascending sensory tracts. Lesions in the pons can cause contralateral hemiparesis and hemisensory loss, but also often involve cranial nerve nuclei, leading to specific cranial nerve deficits (e.g., facial weakness, gaze palsy, ataxia). The patient's symptoms are consistent with a cortical/capsular lesion rather than a brainstem lesion, as there are no specific cranial nerve signs mentioned. The MCA does not typically supply the pons directly.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                 | Location                                  | Function                                                                 | Lesion Effects                                                                                                | Vascular Supply (Relevant) | Relation to Precentral Gyrus/MCA |
| :------------------------ | :---------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | :-------------------------- | :-------------------------------- |
| Internal Capsule (Post. Limb) | Deep white matter, between thalamus & basal ganglia | Pathway for corticospinal & corticobulbar tracts (motor), thalamocortical radiations (sensory) | Contralateral hemiparesis (face, arm, leg), contralateral sensory loss, visual field deficits (optic radiations) | MCA (lenticulostriate branches) | Adjacent to precentral gyrus     |
| Basal Ganglia (Putamen)   | Deep gray matter, lateral to internal capsule | Motor control (initiation, regulation), learning                          | Hypo-/Hyperkinetic disorders, hemiballismus                                                                    | MCA (lenticulostriate branches) | Medial to internal capsule       |
| Thalamus (VPL)            | Deep gray matter, superior to midbrain     | Sensory relay station (body)                                              | Contralateral sensory loss (hemianesthesia)                                                                    | PCA, MCA (thalamogeniculate) | Posterior to internal capsule     |
| Cerebellum (Inf. Hem.)    | Posterior fossa                             | Coordination, balance, motor learning                                     | Ipsilateral ataxia, dysmetria, intention tremor, nystagmus                                                     | AICA, Vertebral A.          | Distal from MCA territory        |
| Brainstem (Pons)          | Between midbrain & medulla                  | Contains motor & sensory tracts, cranial nerve nuclei                      | Contralateral hemiparesis/sensory loss, cranial nerve palsies, ataxia                                          | Basilar A., Vertebral A.     | Distal from MCA territory        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
A stroke affecting the motor cortex (precentral gyrus) often involves the adjacent descending motor pathways within the internal capsule, leading to contralateral hemiparesis. Differentiating lesions based on location (cortex vs. capsule vs. thalamus vs. basal ganglia vs. brainstem) is crucial for understanding the resulting neurological deficits. Remember the classic MCA stroke syndrome involves contralateral motor/sensory deficits (face/arm > leg) and potentially aphasia (if dominant hemisphere) or neglect (if non-dominant hemisphere).

<a id='question-036'></a>

### Question 036: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 36 (constant)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the functional localization within the cerebral cortex, specifically the relationship between the primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus) and the corresponding body parts, and the vascular supply (middle cerebral artery - MCA) to this region. (理解大脑皮层的功能定位，特别是初级运动皮层（中央前回）与相应身体部位之间的关系，以及该区域的血管供应（大脑中动脉 - MCA）。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with sudden onset of right-sided weakness and numbness, predominantly affecting his right face, arm, and hand. His speech is slurred, and he has difficulty understanding simple commands. Neurological examination reveals right hemiparesis (face, arm > leg), right hemisensory loss (face, arm > leg), and expressive aphasia. A non-contrast head CT scan shows no evidence of hemorrhage. MRI of the brain reveals an acute ischemic infarct in the left cerebral hemisphere. Specifically, the MRI shows signal changes consistent with infarction involving the superior portion of the precentral gyrus and the adjacent supramarginal gyrus.

Which of the following structures is MOST likely affected by this infarct, leading to the patient's specific motor deficits?

(A) The primary somatosensory cortex (postcentral gyrus)
(B) The primary visual cortex (occipital lobe)
(C) The primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus)
(D) The supplementary motor area (medial frontal lobe)
(E) The primary auditory cortex (temporal lobe)

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia) presenting with acute onset right-sided weakness (hemiparesis) and numbness (hemisensory loss), affecting the face, arm, and hand more than the leg. Slurred speech (dysarthria) and difficulty understanding commands (receptive aphasia) are also present. This constellation of symptoms strongly suggests a stroke affecting the left cerebral hemisphere.
- **Neurological Examination:** Right hemiparesis (face, arm > leg) and right hemisensory loss (face, arm > leg) indicate damage to the contralateral (left) motor and sensory pathways controlling these body parts. The disproportionate involvement of the face and arm compared to the leg is a key clue.
- **Imaging Findings:** MRI confirms an acute ischemic infarct in the left cerebral hemisphere, specifically involving the superior portion of the precentral gyrus and the adjacent supramarginal gyrus.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the structure MOST likely affected, leading to the specific motor deficits.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Precentral Gyrus:** Located in the frontal lobe, immediately anterior to the central sulcus. It constitutes the primary motor cortex (Brodmann area 4).
- **Motor Homunculus:** The primary motor cortex exhibits somatotopic organization, represented by the motor homunculus. The size of the cortical area dedicated to a body part is proportional to the complexity and precision of its motor control, not its physical size. The face and hand have disproportionately large representations in the motor cortex, located superiorly and laterally. The leg representation is located inferiorly and medially.
- **Clinical Correlation:** An infarct affecting the superior portion of the left precentral gyrus would primarily damage the cortical areas controlling the contralateral (right) face and arm. This perfectly matches the patient's clinical presentation of right hemiparesis affecting the face and arm more than the leg. The supramarginal gyrus involvement might contribute to sensory deficits or language issues (aphasia), but the primary motor deficits point directly to the precentral gyrus.
- **Vascular Supply:** The precentral gyrus is primarily supplied by the middle cerebral artery (MCA), specifically its superior division. An MCA occlusion or branch infarct is a common cause of stroke affecting this region.
- **Embryology:** The cerebral cortex develops from the neural tube, specifically the neural crest cells migrating to form the marginal zone, which later differentiates into the six cortical layers. The precentral gyrus arises from the dorsal portion of the telencephalic vesicle.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **The primary somatosensory cortex (postcentral gyrus):** Located posterior to the central sulcus. While an infarct here could cause contralateral sensory loss (matching the patient's hemisensory loss), it would not directly cause the motor deficits (hemiparesis). The question specifically asks for the structure responsible for the *motor* deficits. The postcentral gyrus is organized somatotopically similarly to the precentral gyrus, but it processes sensory information, not motor commands.
- (B) **The primary visual cortex (occipital lobe):** Located in the posterior aspect of the cerebral hemispheres. Damage here causes visual field deficits (e.g., homonymous hemianopia), not motor or sensory deficits affecting the limbs and face.
- (D) **The supplementary motor area (medial frontal lobe):** Located anterior and superior to the primary motor cortex, on the medial surface of the frontal lobe. It is involved in planning and sequencing complex movements, motor learning, and bimanual coordination. Damage typically causes difficulties with complex motor tasks, apraxia, or impaired gait, rather than the isolated contralateral hemiparesis affecting the face and arm seen in this patient.
- (E) **The primary auditory cortex (temporal lobe):** Located in the superior temporal gyrus. Damage here causes auditory processing deficits, not motor or sensory deficits affecting the limbs and face.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                     | Location                     | Function                                     | Key Clinical Deficit (Lesion)                               | Somatotopic Organization | Vascular Supply (Primary) |
| :--------------------------- | :--------------------------- | :------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------- | :----------------------- |
| Primary Motor Cortex (C)      | Precentral Gyrus (Frontal)   | Execution of voluntary movement               | Contralateral hemiparesis/hemiplegia (face/arm > leg if superior lesion) | Motor Homunculus        | MCA (Superior Division) |
| Primary Somatosensory Cortex (A) | Postcentral Gyrus (Parietal) | Processing touch, temperature, pain, proprioception | Contralateral hemisensory loss (face/arm > leg if superior lesion) | Sensory Homunculus      | MCA (Superior Division) |
| Primary Visual Cortex (B)     | Occipital Lobe               | Processing visual information                 | Contralateral homonymous hemianopia                         | None                    | PCA                     |
| Supplementary Motor Area (D) | Medial Frontal Lobe          | Planning/sequencing movement, motor learning | Apraxia, difficulty with complex movements                 | None                    | ACA                     |
| Primary Auditory Cortex (E)   | Temporal Lobe                | Processing auditory information               | Auditory agnosia, impaired sound localization               | None                    | MCA (Inferior Division) |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus) controls voluntary movement, exhibiting somatotopic organization (motor homunculus) where the face and hand have large representations superiorly. Lesions here cause contralateral motor deficits corresponding to the affected cortical area. Differentiating the precentral gyrus (motor) from the postcentral gyrus (sensory) based on clinical deficits is crucial. Remember the vascular territories (MCA for pre/postcentral gyrus, PCA for occipital, ACA for medial frontal/parietal).

<a id='question-037'></a>

### Question 037: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 37 (humans.)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the functional localization within the cerebral cortex, specifically the relationship between the primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus) and the internal capsule, and how lesions in these areas manifest clinically. (理解大脑皮层的功能定位，特别是初级运动皮层（中央前回）与内囊之间的关系，以及这些区域病变如何表现出临床症状。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with sudden onset of right-sided weakness and numbness. The symptoms began approximately 2 hours ago while he was watching television. On examination, his vital signs are stable. Neurological examination reveals right hemiparesis (strength 3/5 in the right upper and lower extremities), right hemisensory loss (decreased sensation to light touch and pinprick on the right side of the body), and mild dysarthria. Reflexes are hyperactive on the right side. Cranial nerves are intact. An emergent non-contrast head CT scan shows a small area of hypodensity in the left basal ganglia, suggestive of an acute ischemic stroke. Given the patient's presentation and imaging findings, which of the following structures is most likely affected, leading to the observed contralateral motor and sensory deficits?

(A) Left superior temporal gyrus
(B) Left inferior frontal gyrus (Broca's area)
(C) Left precentral gyrus
(D) Left internal capsule (posterior limb)
(E) Left cerebellum

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia). This increases the likelihood of a stroke.
- **Onset:** Sudden onset of right-sided weakness and numbness suggests a vascular event (stroke).
- **Symptoms:** Right hemiparesis (weakness) and right hemisensory loss (numbness) indicate a lesion affecting the motor and sensory pathways controlling the right side of the body. The contralateral nature points to a left-sided lesion.
- **Neurological Exam:** Right hemiparesis (3/5 strength), right hemisensory loss, hyperreflexia on the right, and dysarthria are consistent with an upper motor neuron lesion affecting the corticospinal and spinothalamic tracts.
- **Imaging:** CT scan shows a left basal ganglia lesion, which is a common location for lacunar strokes affecting deep structures.
- **Question:** Asks for the structure most likely affected causing the contralateral motor and sensory deficits.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Motor Pathway:** The primary motor cortex, located in the precentral gyrus (part of the frontal lobe), controls voluntary movement. Corticospinal tract fibers originate here and descend through the internal capsule (specifically the posterior limb) before decussating in the medulla.
- **Sensory Pathway:** The primary somatosensory cortex, located in the postcentral gyrus (part of the parietal lobe), receives sensory information. Spinothalamic tract fibers ascend through the internal capsule (specifically the posterior limb) before synapsing in the thalamus and projecting to the somatosensory cortex.
- **Internal Capsule:** This is a white matter structure deep within the cerebral hemispheres, situated between the thalamus and the basal ganglia. It contains ascending sensory fibers (including spinothalamic) and descending motor fibers (including corticospinal). The posterior limb is particularly dense with these tracts.
- **Lesion Location:** A stroke affecting the left basal ganglia region can easily extend into or directly involve the adjacent left internal capsule (posterior limb).
- **Clinical Correlation:** A lesion in the left internal capsule (posterior limb) would disrupt both the descending corticospinal tracts (causing right hemiparesis) and the ascending spinothalamic tracts (causing right hemisensory loss). The dysarthria could result from involvement of corticobulbar tracts also passing through the internal capsule or from involvement of nearby structures. The hyperreflexia is characteristic of an upper motor neuron lesion.
- **Differential Diagnosis:**
    - **Precentral Gyrus (C):** A lesion here would cause contralateral motor deficits (hemiparesis) but typically spares sensation.
    - **Superior Temporal Gyrus (A):** Primarily involved in auditory processing and language comprehension (Wernicke's area). Lesions cause auditory deficits or receptive aphasia, not contralateral motor/sensory loss.
    - **Inferior Frontal Gyrus (B):** Contains Broca's area (motor speech production). Lesions cause expressive aphasia, not contralateral motor/sensory loss.
    - **Cerebellum (E):** Primarily involved in coordination, balance, and motor learning. Lesions cause ataxia, dysmetria, intention tremor, and nystagmus, not contralateral hemiparesis/hemisensory loss.

##### 3. Embryological Origin:** The cerebral cortex develops from the neural tube via neurogenesis and neuronal migration. The internal capsule forms as white matter tracts myelinate and connect different cortical areas and subcortical structures.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Left superior temporal gyrus:** This area is primarily involved in auditory processing (primary auditory cortex) and language comprehension (Wernicke's area). A lesion here would typically cause auditory hallucinations, difficulty understanding language (receptive aphasia), or deficits in sound localization, not contralateral motor and sensory deficits. The patient's symptoms are clearly motor and sensory, ruling out this option.
- (B) **Left inferior frontal gyrus (Broca's area):** This area is crucial for motor speech production. A lesion here typically causes expressive aphasia (difficulty forming words and sentences), although it can sometimes cause contralateral facial/arm weakness if the lesion extends to involve the motor cortex. However, it would not explain the widespread right hemiparesis and hemisensory loss seen in this patient. The primary deficit is motor/sensory, not speech production.
- (C) **Left precentral gyrus:** This is the location of the primary motor cortex. A lesion here would cause contralateral motor deficits (right hemiparesis), but it would typically spare sensory function. The patient has both motor and sensory deficits, making a lesion confined solely to the precentral gyrus less likely than a lesion affecting a structure containing both motor and sensory tracts.
- (E) **Left cerebellum:** The cerebellum is involved in motor coordination, balance, and posture. Lesions typically cause ataxia (uncoordinated movements), dysmetria (inability to judge distance), intention tremor, nystagmus, and gait disturbances. While cerebellar strokes can occur, they do not cause contralateral hemiparesis and hemisensory loss, which are signs of corticospinal and spinothalamic tract involvement, respectively.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                     | Location                                  | Primary Function(s)                                  | Typical Lesion Symptoms                                                                 | Relevance to Case                                                                                                |
| :--------------------------- | :---------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Precentral Gyrus (C)**     | Frontal lobe, anterior to central sulcus   | Primary motor cortex (voluntary movement)             | Contralateral hemiparesis/hemiplegia, spasticity, hyperreflexia                          | Explains motor deficit but not sensory deficit.                                                                   |
| **Postcentral Gyrus**        | Parietal lobe, posterior to central sulcus  | Primary somatosensory cortex (touch, pain, temp)      | Contralateral hemisensory loss                                                           | Explains sensory deficit but not motor deficit.                                                                    |
| **Superior Temporal Gyrus (A)** | Temporal lobe, superior surface            | Auditory processing, language comprehension (Wernicke's) | Auditory deficits, receptive aphasia                                                     | Does not explain motor or sensory deficits.                                                                      |
| **Inferior Frontal Gyrus (B)** | Frontal lobe, inferior surface            | Motor speech production (Broca's area)               | Expressive aphasia, possible contralateral facial/arm weakness if lesion extends laterally | Does not explain widespread motor/sensory deficits.                                                               |
| **Internal Capsule (D)**      | Deep white matter, between thalamus & BG | Contains corticospinal, spinothalamic, corticobulbar tracts | Contralateral hemiparesis/hemisensory loss, possible dysarthria/dysphagia              | **Best fits** the patient's presentation of contralateral motor and sensory deficits due to involvement of multiple tracts. |
| **Cerebellum (E)**           | Posterior fossa, inferior to occipital lobes | Coordination, balance, motor learning                | Ataxia, dysmetria, intention tremor, nystagmus, gait disturbance                       | Does not explain contralateral hemiparesis/hemisensory loss.                                                        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
A lesion in the internal capsule, particularly the posterior limb, is a classic cause of contralateral hemiparesis and hemisensory loss due to the dense packing of ascending sensory and descending motor tracts within this structure. Differentiating lesions based on the specific tracts involved (motor vs. sensory vs. language vs. visual) is crucial for neurological localization. Remember that the internal capsule is a common site for lacunar infarcts.

<a id='question-038'></a>

### Question 038: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 38 (prominent)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Identify the specific cortical area (Part 1 Focus 38) and its associated function based on a clinical presentation involving sensory processing and integration, specifically related to the posterior parietal lobe and its connections. (识别皮层区域（Part 1 Focus 38）及其相关功能，基于涉及后顶叶及其连接的感觉处理和整合的临床表现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old right-handed male with a history of hypertension presents to the emergency department after experiencing sudden onset weakness and sensory loss on his left side. Neurological examination reveals right homonymous hemianopia, left-sided sensory neglect (anosognosia for left hemiplegia), left-sided hemiparesis (affecting face, arm, and leg), left-sided hemihypoesthesia (affecting face, arm, and leg), and left-sided astereognosis (inability to identify objects by touch). MRI of the brain shows an acute ischemic infarct in the right posterior parietal lobe, sparing the occipital lobe and temporal lobe. The patient is unable to copy a drawing of interlocking pentagons accurately, omitting the left side of the figure. Which cortical area, corresponding to Part 1 Focus 38 in the prominent view of the cerebral cortex, is most likely affected by this infarct?

(A) Primary Somatosensory Cortex (S1)
(B) Primary Visual Cortex (V1)
(C) Primary Motor Cortex (M1)
(D) Superior Parietal Lobule
(E) Primary Auditory Cortex (A1)

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male, sudden onset left-sided weakness and sensory loss, history of hypertension (risk factor for stroke).
- **Neurological Findings:**
    - Right homonymous hemianopia: Suggests involvement of the visual pathway, likely posterior to the optic chiasm, potentially affecting the optic radiations passing near the parietal lobe or occipital lobe. However, the MRI specifies sparing of the occipital lobe.
    - Left-sided sensory neglect (anosognosia for left hemiplegia): A classic sign of right parietal lobe damage, specifically affecting the non-dominant hemisphere's role in spatial awareness and attention to the contralateral side.
    - Left-sided hemiparesis (face, arm, leg): Indicates damage to the motor pathways controlling the left side of the body, likely involving the right motor cortex or descending pathways.
    - Left-sided hemihypoesthesia (face, arm, leg): Indicates damage to the sensory pathways carrying information from the left side of the body, likely involving the right sensory cortex or ascending pathways.
    - Left-sided astereognosis: Inability to recognize objects by touch, strongly implicating the parietal lobe, specifically the somatosensory association cortex.
    - Inability to copy interlocking pentagons (omitting the left side): A specific test for visuospatial constructional apraxia, highly indicative of right parietal lobe dysfunction.
- **MRI Findings:** Acute ischemic infarct in the *right posterior parietal lobe*, sparing occipital and temporal lobes.
- **Question Stem:** Asks to identify the specific cortical area (Part 1 Focus 38) affected.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Part 1 Focus 38 (Prominent View):** This refers to a specific area highlighted in standard anatomical diagrams of the cerebral cortex, typically focusing on the lateral surface. In the prominent view, the posterior parietal lobe is a key region.
- **Right Posterior Parietal Lobe:** This area contains several functionally distinct subregions:
    - **Superior Parietal Lobule (SPL):** Involved in spatial processing, attention, visuomotor integration, and sensory integration. Damage here classically causes contralateral sensory neglect, astereognosis, apraxia, and difficulties with spatial tasks like copying figures. The SPL is a major component of Part 1 Focus 38 in the prominent view.
    - **Inferior Parietal Lobule (IPL):** Contains the supramarginal gyrus and angular gyrus. Involved in language processing (angular gyrus), number processing, and integrating sensory information.
    - **Primary Somatosensory Cortex (S1):** Located in the precentral gyrus (postcentral gyrus in the parietal lobe), receives primary tactile information. Damage causes contralateral sensory loss.
    - **Primary Visual Cortex (V1):** Located in the occipital lobe, receives primary visual input. Damage causes contralateral homonymous hemianopia.
    - **Primary Motor Cortex (M1):** Located in the precentral gyrus (frontal lobe), controls voluntary movement. Damage causes contralateral hemiparesis.
    - **Primary Auditory Cortex (A1):** Located in the temporal lobe (Heschl's gyrus), processes auditory information.
- **Correlation with Symptoms:** The patient's symptoms (left sensory neglect, astereognosis, constructional apraxia, left hemiparesis, left hemihypoesthesia, right homonymous hemianopia) point to damage in the right posterior parietal lobe. The specific combination of neglect, astereognosis, and constructional apraxia strongly implicates the *superior parietal lobule* (SPL), which is a prominent feature in Part 1 Focus 38. While S1 is also in the parietal lobe and contributes to sensory loss, the neglect, astereognosis, and apraxia are more characteristic of the association cortex within the SPL. The homonymous hemianopia suggests involvement of optic radiations, which pass near the parietal lobe, but the MRI specifies sparing of the occipital lobe, making direct V1 involvement unlikely. The hemiparesis suggests involvement of M1 or descending motor tracts, but the primary deficit described is sensory and spatial. The infarct location specified in the MRI (right posterior parietal lobe) directly corresponds to the anatomical location of the SPL.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Primary Somatosensory Cortex (S1):** Located in the postcentral gyrus of the parietal lobe. Damage causes contralateral sensory loss (hemihypoesthesia), which the patient has. However, S1 damage alone does not typically cause sensory neglect, astereognosis, or constructional apraxia. These higher-order functions are associated with the parietal association cortex (SPL). While S1 is part of the parietal lobe, the constellation of symptoms points more specifically to the SPL.
- (B) **Primary Visual Cortex (V1):** Located in the occipital lobe. Damage causes contralateral homonymous hemianopia. The MRI explicitly states the occipital lobe is spared, ruling out direct V1 damage as the primary cause of the symptoms described. While optic radiations pass near the parietal lobe, the core deficits (neglect, astereognosis, apraxia) are not primarily visual.
- (C) **Primary Motor Cortex (M1):** Located in the precentral gyrus of the frontal lobe. Damage causes contralateral hemiparesis, which the patient has. However, M1 damage does not cause sensory neglect, astereognosis, hemihypoesthesia, or visual field defects. The infarct is located in the parietal lobe, not the frontal lobe.
- (E) **Primary Auditory Cortex (A1):** Located in the temporal lobe. Damage causes auditory deficits. The patient's symptoms are primarily sensory, motor, and visuospatial, with no mention of auditory problems. The infarct is in the parietal lobe, not the temporal lobe.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                     | Location             | Primary Function                                     | Key Symptoms of Damage                                                                                                | Relation to Part 1 Focus 38 |
| :--------------------------- | :------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------- |
| **Superior Parietal Lobule (D)** | Posterior Parietal Lobe | Spatial processing, attention, sensory integration | Contralateral sensory neglect, astereognosis, apraxia, constructional apraxia, visuomotor integration deficits        | **Prominent Feature**      |
| Primary Somatosensory Cortex (A) | Postcentral Gyrus (Parietal Lobe) | Processing touch, temperature, pain, proprioception | Contralateral sensory loss (touch, pain, temp, proprioception)                                                       | Part of Parietal Lobe      |
| Primary Visual Cortex (B)      | Occipital Lobe       | Processing visual information                         | Contralateral homonymous hemianopia                                                                                   | Not in Parietal Lobe       |
| Primary Motor Cortex (C)       | Precentral Gyrus (Frontal Lobe) | Voluntary motor control                               | Contralateral hemiparesis/hemiplegia                                                                                 | Not in Parietal Lobe       |
| Primary Auditory Cortex (E)    | Temporal Lobe        | Processing auditory information                       | Auditory agnosia, hearing loss                                                                                        | Not in Parietal Lobe       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The right posterior parietal lobe, particularly the superior parietal lobule (Part 1 Focus 38), is crucial for integrating sensory information, spatial awareness, and attention. Damage here classically leads to contralateral sensory neglect, astereognosis, and constructional apraxia. Differentiating parietal lobe lesions from occipital (visual field defects), frontal (motor deficits), and temporal (auditory/language deficits) lesions is key. Remember that S1 is also in the parietal lobe but primarily handles primary sensory input, whereas the SPL handles higher-order sensory integration and spatial processing.
