<a id='question-047'></a>

### Question 047: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 47 (separates)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The central sulcus (大脑裂) separates the frontal lobe (额叶) from the parietal lobe (顶叶), and the parieto-occipital sulcus (顶枕裂) separates the parietal lobe from the occipital lobe (枕叶). Understanding these sulci is crucial for localizing cortical function and identifying lesions.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with acute onset of right-sided weakness and sensory loss affecting his left face, arm, and leg. He also exhibits expressive aphasia (difficulty speaking) and right-sided neglect (inattention to the right side of space). A non-contrast head CT scan reveals a large left middle cerebral artery (MCA) territory infarct. Neurological examination confirms right hemiparesis (face, arm > leg), right hemisensory loss (face, arm > leg), right facial droop, expressive aphasia, and right hemispatial neglect. The patient is unable to accurately copy a drawing of intersecting pentagons, consistently omitting the left side of the figure. Which anatomical landmark on the cerebral cortex is most likely involved in the patient's right hemispatial neglect?

(A) Lateral Sulcus (Sylvian Fissure)
(B) Central Sulcus (Rolanic Fissure)
(C) Parieto-occipital Sulcus (Calcarine Fissure)
(D) Longitudinal Fissure
(E) Temporal Sulcus

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male, history of vascular risk factors (HTN, hyperlipidemia), acute onset right hemiparesis/hemisensory loss (left face, arm > leg), expressive aphasia, right hemispatial neglect.
- **Imaging:** Left MCA infarct.
- **Neurological Exam:** Right hemiparesis, hemisensory loss, facial droop, expressive aphasia, right hemispatial neglect, difficulty copying left side of intersecting pentagons (a classic test for neglect).
- **Key Clue:** The combination of right hemispatial neglect and a left MCA infarct points to damage in the left parietal lobe, specifically the posterior parietal cortex. The question asks for the anatomical landmark separating the parietal lobe from the occipital lobe, which is the parieto-occipital sulcus. The test for copying intersecting pentagons specifically assesses visuospatial attention, often impaired in parietal lobe lesions.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Cerebral Cortex Lobes & Sulci:** The cerebral cortex is divided into four main lobes: frontal, parietal, temporal, and occipital. These lobes are demarcated by deep sulci.
    - **Longitudinal Fissure (A):** Separates the left and right cerebral hemispheres. Not relevant to lobe separation within a hemisphere.
    - **Central Sulcus (B):** Also known as the Rolandic fissure, separates the frontal lobe (precentral gyrus - motor cortex) from the parietal lobe (postcentral gyrus - somatosensory cortex). Damage here typically causes motor or sensory deficits, not primarily neglect.
    - **Lateral Sulcus (A):** Also known as the Sylvian fissure, separates the frontal and parietal lobes superiorly from the temporal lobe inferiorly. Damage here can affect various functions depending on the specific location, but neglect is less commonly associated with primary lesions confined to this sulcus itself.
    - **Parieto-occipital Sulcus (C):** Separates the parietal lobe from the occipital lobe. The posterior parietal cortex, particularly the angular gyrus and superior parietal lobule, is crucial for spatial awareness, attention, and integration of sensory information. Lesions in this area, especially in the non-dominant (usually left) hemisphere, commonly cause contralateral hemispatial neglect. The parieto-occipital sulcus is also sometimes referred to as the calcarine fissure, although the calcarine fissure specifically refers to the sulcus separating the superior and inferior parts of the occipital lobe, containing the primary visual cortex (V1). However, in the context of separating the parietal and occipital lobes, parieto-occipital sulcus is the more accurate term.
    - **Temporal Sulcus (E):** Separates the superior temporal gyrus from the middle temporal gyrus. It is located within the temporal lobe. Damage here is associated with auditory processing deficits or Wernicke's aphasia (if involving the posterior superior temporal gyrus), not typically neglect.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's left MCA infarct affects the left hemisphere, including the left parietal lobe. The right hemispatial neglect is a classic sign of damage to the left posterior parietal cortex. This region is responsible for processing spatial information and directing attention. The parieto-occipital sulcus forms the posterior boundary of the parietal lobe.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Lateral Sulcus (Sylvian Fissure):** Separates the temporal lobe from the frontal and parietal lobes. Lesions involving the Sylvian fissure area can cause various deficits (e.g., sensory deficits, aphasia if involving Broca's or Wernicke's areas), but contralateral neglect is not the primary or most characteristic symptom associated with damage specifically to this sulcus itself.
- (B) **Central Sulcus (Rolanic Fissure):** Separates the frontal lobe (motor cortex) from the parietal lobe (somatosensory cortex). Lesions here typically cause contralateral motor weakness (if precentral gyrus) or sensory loss (if postcentral gyrus). While the parietal lobe is involved, the central sulcus itself is not the primary site associated with neglect.
- (D) **Longitudinal Fissure:** Separates the two cerebral hemispheres. Damage to this fissure is not a typical clinical entity and does not relate to lobe separation within a hemisphere or specific cortical functions like spatial attention.
- (E) **Temporal Sulcus:** Located within the temporal lobe, separating the superior and middle temporal gyri. Lesions here are associated with auditory processing deficits or Wernicke's aphasia, not contralateral neglect.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                 | Location                                  | Separates                                      | Primary Function/Clinical Correlation                                                                 | Relevance to Neglect                                                                 |
| :------------------------ | :---------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Parieto-occipital Sulcus** | Posterior boundary of parietal lobe       | **Parietal Lobe** from **Occipital Lobe**       | Boundary between association cortex (parietal) and primary visual cortex (occipital)                  | **High:** Lesions in adjacent posterior parietal cortex cause contralateral neglect. |
| Central Sulcus            | Between frontal and parietal lobes        | **Frontal Lobe** from **Parietal Lobe**       | Motor cortex (precentral) vs. Somatosensory cortex (postcentral)                                    | Low: Primarily motor/sensory deficits.                                                |
| Lateral Sulcus            | Between temporal and other lobes          | **Temporal Lobe** from **Frontal/Parietal Lobes** | Separates temporal lobe; contains structures like insula.                                           | Low: Various deficits, but neglect less common.                                       |
| Longitudinal Fissure      | Between cerebral hemispheres              | **Left Hemisphere** from **Right Hemisphere**   | Divides the brain into two halves.                                                                  | None: Not related to cortical lobe function or neglect.                               |
| Temporal Sulcus           | Within the temporal lobe                  | Superior temporal gyrus from middle temporal gyrus | Auditory processing, language comprehension (Wernicke's area).                                      | None: Primarily auditory/language deficits.                                           |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The parieto-occipital sulcus demarcates the parietal and occipital lobes. Lesions in the non-dominant (usually left) posterior parietal lobe, often due to MCA stroke, are a common cause of contralateral hemispatial neglect. Differentiating this sulcus from the central sulcus (motor/sensory), lateral sulcus (temporal lobe separation), and longitudinal fissure (hemisphere separation) is crucial for localizing neurological deficits.

<a id='question-048'></a>

### Question 048: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 48 (frontal)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The clinical presentation of impaired executive function, personality changes, and motor deficits (specifically affecting the contralateral lower limb) points to damage in the frontal lobe, specifically involving the prefrontal cortex and potentially the motor cortex or underlying white matter tracts. This tests knowledge of frontal lobe anatomy, function, and vascular supply. (额叶功能障碍，执行功能受损，人格改变，对侧下肢运动障碍，提示额叶损伤，特别是前额叶皮层和/或运动皮层及其相关通路。考察额叶解剖、功能和血管供应知识。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department after experiencing sudden onset of severe headache, right-sided weakness (predominantly affecting the right leg), and difficulty planning and organizing tasks. His speech is fluent but lacks content and shows impaired judgment. He exhibits disinhibition and inappropriate social behavior. Neurological examination reveals right hemiparesis (strength 2/5 in the right leg, 4/5 in the right arm and face), right facial droop, and impaired tandem gait. Sensory examination is intact. Reflexes are brisk on the right side. Cranial nerve examination is otherwise normal except for the facial droop. An emergent non-contrast head CT scan reveals a large left middle cerebral artery (MCA) territory infarct involving the frontal and parietal lobes.

Which area of the cerebral cortex is MOST likely affected, explaining the patient's profound changes in personality, executive function, and contralateral lower limb weakness?

(A) Primary Somatosensory Cortex (Brodmann areas 3, 1, 2)
(B) Primary Motor Cortex (Brodmann area 4)
(C) Prefrontal Cortex (Brodmann areas 9, 10, 11, 46, 47)
(D) Primary Visual Cortex (Brodmann area 17)
(E) Primary Auditory Cortex (Brodmann areas 41, 42)

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia). This increases the likelihood of a stroke.
- **Acute Onset:** Sudden severe headache, weakness, and cognitive changes strongly suggest a cerebrovascular event (stroke).
- **Neurological Deficits:**
    - **Right Hemiparesis (Leg > Arm > Face):** Indicates damage to the left motor cortex or descending motor pathways (corticospinal tract). The leg > arm > face pattern is characteristic of involvement of the medial aspect of the motor cortex (leg area) or the internal capsule.
    - **Right Facial Droop:** Suggests involvement of the left facial motor cortex or corticobulbar tract.
    - **Impaired Executive Function (planning, organizing):** Points to dysfunction in the prefrontal cortex.
    - **Personality Changes (disinhibition, inappropriate behavior):** Strongly associated with damage to the prefrontal cortex, particularly the orbitofrontal region.
    - **Fluent but Impoverished Speech (lack of content, impaired judgment):** Suggests involvement of the prefrontal cortex affecting language processing and social cognition.
- **Imaging:** Left MCA territory infarct involving frontal and parietal lobes. The MCA supplies the lateral surface of the frontal, parietal, and temporal lobes. The infarct location correlates well with the observed deficits.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the specific cortical area responsible for the combination of personality/executive changes and contralateral motor weakness.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Frontal Lobe Anatomy:** The frontal lobe is the largest lobe of the brain, located anteriorly. It is divided into several functional areas based on Brodmann cytoarchitecture.
    - **Prefrontal Cortex (Brodmann areas 9, 10, 11, 46, 47):** Located in the most anterior part of the frontal lobe. It is crucial for executive functions (planning, decision-making, working memory), personality, social behavior, and impulse control. Damage here leads to personality changes, disinhibition, impaired judgment, and executive dysfunction, as seen in this patient.
    - **Primary Motor Cortex (Brodmann area 4):** Located in the precentral gyrus. It controls voluntary movements. Damage causes contralateral weakness (hemiparesis). The motor homunculus shows a large representation for the leg and face, located medially, and a smaller representation for the arm, located laterally. An MCA stroke affecting the medial aspect of the motor cortex would preferentially affect the leg.
    - **Premotor Cortex (Brodmann area 6) and Supplementary Motor Area (SMA):** Located anterior to the primary motor cortex. Involved in planning and sequencing movements.
    - **Broca's Area (Brodmann areas 44, 45):** Typically located in the inferior frontal gyrus of the dominant hemisphere. Involved in speech production. Damage causes expressive aphasia.
    - **Primary Somatosensory Cortex (Brodmann areas 3, 1, 2):** Located in the postcentral gyrus. Processes sensory information (touch, pain, temperature, proprioception). Damage causes contralateral sensory loss.
    - **Primary Visual Cortex (Brodmann area 17):** Located in the occipital lobe. Processes visual information. Damage causes visual field defects.
    - **Primary Auditory Cortex (Brodmann areas 41, 42):** Located in the temporal lobe (superior temporal gyrus). Processes auditory information. Damage causes auditory deficits.
- **MCA Stroke:** The MCA supplies the lateral surface of the cerebral hemispheres, including most of the frontal, parietal, and temporal lobes. A stroke in the left MCA territory commonly affects the language areas (Broca's, Wernicke's) and the motor/sensory cortex controlling the right side of the body.
- **Correlation:** The patient's symptoms (personality changes, executive dysfunction, right hemiparesis with leg predominance) are best explained by damage to the left frontal lobe, specifically involving both the prefrontal cortex (for cognitive/behavioral changes) and the motor cortex (for motor weakness). The prefrontal cortex (Brodmann areas 9, 10, 11, 46, 47) is the primary site responsible for the observed personality and executive function deficits. The motor cortex (Brodmann area 4), particularly its medial portion representing the leg, is responsible for the contralateral leg weakness. Since the infarct involves the frontal lobe and the question asks for the area explaining *both* personality/executive changes *and* motor weakness, the prefrontal cortex is the most encompassing answer for the cognitive/behavioral aspects, while the motor cortex explains the motor deficit. However, the profound personality and executive changes are hallmark signs of prefrontal cortex damage.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Primary Somatosensory Cortex (Brodmann areas 3, 1, 2):** Located in the postcentral gyrus. Damage causes contralateral sensory loss (touch, pain, temperature, proprioception). This patient has intact sensation. Incorrect.
- (B) **Primary Motor Cortex (Brodmann area 4):** Located in the precentral gyrus. Damage causes contralateral weakness (hemiparesis). While this patient has right hemiparesis, this option does not fully explain the profound personality and executive function changes. The prefrontal cortex is more directly responsible for these cognitive/behavioral deficits. While the motor cortex is involved in the weakness, the question asks for the area explaining *all* major symptoms, especially the prominent cognitive/behavioral ones.
- (D) **Primary Visual Cortex (Brodmann area 17):** Located in the occipital lobe. Damage causes visual field defects (hemianopia, quadrantanopia). This patient has no reported visual symptoms. Incorrect.
- (E) **Primary Auditory Cortex (Brodmann areas 41, 42):** Located in the temporal lobe. Damage causes auditory processing deficits. This patient has no reported auditory symptoms. Incorrect.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature                 | Prefrontal Cortex (Brodmann 9, 10, 11, 46, 47) | Primary Motor Cortex (Brodmann 4) | Primary Somatosensory Cortex (Brodmann 3, 1, 2) | Primary Visual Cortex (Brodmann 17) | Primary Auditory Cortex (Brodmann 41, 42) |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------- |
| **Location**            | Anterior Frontal Lobe                           | Precentral Gyrus                  | Postcentral Gyrus                               | Occipital Lobe                       | Temporal Lobe (Superior Temporal Gyrus) |
| **Primary Function**    | Executive Function, Personality, Behavior       | Voluntary Motor Control           | Sensory Perception                             | Visual Processing                    | Auditory Processing                     |
| **Deficit on Damage**   | Personality Change, Disinhibition, Poor Planning | Contralateral Weakness (Hemiparesis) | Contralateral Sensory Loss                      | Visual Field Defect                  | Auditory Deficit                        |
| **Relevant to Vignette** | Yes (Personality, Executive Function)           | Yes (Motor Weakness)               | No (Sensation Intact)                           | No (No Visual Symptoms)              | No (No Auditory Symptoms)               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The frontal lobe, particularly the prefrontal cortex, is responsible for higher-order cognitive functions, personality, and behavior. Damage to this area, often due to stroke (like an MCA infarct), results in characteristic changes in personality, executive function, and social behavior. Motor deficits (hemiparesis) result from damage to the primary motor cortex, also located in the frontal lobe. Differentiating deficits based on cortical location is crucial for neurological localization. Key differentials include parietal lobe lesions (sensory deficits, neglect), temporal lobe lesions (memory, auditory deficits, language comprehension), and occipital lobe lesions (visual deficits).

<a id='question-049'></a>

### Question 049: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 49 (Temporal)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the functional localization within the temporal lobe, specifically the auditory cortex (Heschl's gyrus) and its vascular supply (middle cerebral artery, specifically the superior division), and correlating lesions with specific deficits. (理解颞叶的功能定位，特别是听觉皮层（海氏裂）及其血管供应（中脑动脉，特别是上皮层分支），并将病变与特定缺陷相关联。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with acute onset of right-sided weakness and difficulty understanding spoken language. He reports that he woke up this morning with these symptoms. Neurological examination reveals right hemiparesis (strength 3/5 in the right upper and lower extremities), right facial droop, and impaired comprehension of complex commands (Wernicke's aphasia). Sensory examination is intact. Cranial nerves II, III, IV, V, VI, VII, VIII, IX, X, XI, and XII are grossly intact, although subtle right peripheral facial palsy is noted. An emergent non-contrast CT scan of the head shows a left middle cerebral artery (MCA) territory infarct. MRI confirms an acute ischemic stroke involving the left superior temporal gyrus, including Heschl's gyrus, and extending into the underlying white matter. Which of the following structures is most likely directly affected by this infarct, leading to the patient's specific auditory processing deficits?

(A) Primary Motor Cortex (Brodmann area 4)
(B) Primary Visual Cortex (Brodmann area 17)
(C) Primary Auditory Cortex (Brodmann area 41)
(D) Broca's Area (Brodmann areas 44 and 45)
(E) Wernicke's Area (Brodmann areas 21, 22, 39, 40)

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia) presenting with acute neurological deficits (right hemiparesis, right facial droop, Wernicke's aphasia). This points towards an acute stroke.
- **Neurological Examination:** Right hemiparesis and facial droop indicate involvement of the left motor cortex or its descending pathways (corticospinal tract). Wernicke's aphasia (impaired language comprehension) points towards involvement of the left posterior superior temporal gyrus.
- **Imaging Findings:** Non-contrast CT and MRI confirm a left MCA territory infarct involving the left superior temporal gyrus, including Heschl's gyrus.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which structure is *most likely directly affected* by the infarct, leading to *specific auditory processing deficits*. The infarct location is given as the superior temporal gyrus, including Heschl's gyrus. Heschl's gyrus is the location of the primary auditory cortex. The question specifically asks about auditory deficits.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Location:** The superior temporal gyrus is part of the temporal lobe, located superior to the Sylvian fissure.
- **Functional Anatomy:** The superior temporal gyrus contains several functionally distinct areas. The most posterior part, known as Heschl's gyrus (primary auditory cortex, Brodmann area 41), is responsible for processing basic auditory information (tonotopy). Anterior to Heschl's gyrus are Wernicke's area (Brodmann areas 21, 22, 39, 40), involved in language comprehension, and other auditory association areas.
- **Vascular Supply:** The superior temporal gyrus is primarily supplied by branches of the middle cerebral artery (MCA), specifically the superior division. The superior division gives off the superior temporal artery, which supplies the superior temporal gyrus, including Heschl's gyrus and Wernicke's area.
- **Clinical Correlation:** An infarct in the left MCA territory affecting the superior temporal gyrus, including Heschl's gyrus, would directly damage the primary auditory cortex. This would lead to auditory processing deficits, such as difficulty discriminating sounds, understanding speech (especially if Wernicke's area is also involved, as stated in the vignette), and potentially auditory agnosia. The vignette explicitly mentions the involvement of Heschl's gyrus in the MRI findings. Wernicke's aphasia is also present, indicating involvement of Wernicke's area, which is located anterior to Heschl's gyrus within the superior temporal gyrus. The question asks for the structure responsible for *auditory processing deficits*, and Heschl's gyrus (primary auditory cortex) is the primary site for this function.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Primary Motor Cortex (Brodmann area 4):** Located in the precentral gyrus of the frontal lobe. Damage causes contralateral motor deficits (hemiparesis/hemiplegia), which the patient has (right hemiparesis due to left MCA infarct), but it is not involved in auditory processing. The infarct location described is in the temporal lobe, not the frontal lobe.
- (B) **Primary Visual Cortex (Brodmann area 17):** Located in the occipital lobe. Damage causes contralateral visual field defects (hemianopia). This is not consistent with the patient's symptoms or the infarct location.
- (D) **Broca's Area (Brodmann areas 44 and 45):** Located in the inferior frontal gyrus (usually left hemisphere). Damage causes expressive aphasia (difficulty producing speech), not receptive aphasia (Wernicke's aphasia) or primary auditory processing deficits. While the MCA infarct can affect Broca's area, the vignette specifically mentions the superior temporal gyrus and Heschl's gyrus, and the patient has Wernicke's aphasia, pointing away from Broca's area as the primary site for the *auditory* deficits.
- (E) **Wernicke's Area (Brodmann areas 21, 22, 39, 40):** Located in the posterior superior temporal gyrus (part of the superior temporal gyrus). Damage causes receptive aphasia (Wernicke's aphasia), which the patient has. While Wernicke's area is involved in language comprehension and is located in the superior temporal gyrus, it is an *association* area, not the *primary* auditory cortex. Heschl's gyrus (primary auditory cortex, Brodmann area 41) is located just posterior to Wernicke's area and is responsible for the initial processing of auditory information. The question asks for the structure responsible for *auditory processing deficits*, and the primary auditory cortex (Heschl's gyrus) is the most direct answer, especially given its explicit mention in the MRI findings.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                     | Location                                  | Function                                      | Brodmann Area(s) | Vascular Supply (MCA) | Clinical Deficit (Lesion)                               |
| :--------------------------- | :---------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------- | :--------------- | :--------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------ |
| Primary Auditory Cortex      | Posterior Superior Temporal Gyrus (Heschl's Gyrus) | Initial processing of auditory information | 41               | Superior Temporal Artery | Auditory Agnosia, Difficulty discriminating sounds      |
| Wernicke's Area              | Posterior Superior Temporal Gyrus         | Language Comprehension                        | 21, 22, 39, 40   | Superior Temporal Artery | Receptive Aphasia (Wernicke's Aphasia)                  |
| Primary Motor Cortex         | Precentral Gyrus (Frontal Lobe)           | Voluntary Motor Control                        | 4                | Central Sulcus Branch  | Contralateral Hemiparesis/Hemiplegia                     |
| Primary Visual Cortex        | Occipital Lobe                             | Visual Processing                              | 17               | Calcarine Artery       | Contralateral Homonymous Hemianopia                      |
| Broca's Area                 | Inferior Frontal Gyrus (Frontal Lobe)    | Speech Production                              | 44, 45           | Frontal Branch         | Expressive Aphasia (Broca's Aphasia)                    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The primary auditory cortex (Heschl's gyrus, Brodmann area 41) is located in the posterior part of the superior temporal gyrus and is responsible for the initial processing of auditory information. Lesions here cause auditory deficits. Wernicke's area, located anterior to Heschl's gyrus in the superior temporal gyrus, is crucial for language comprehension. Differentiating between primary sensory cortex (Brodmann 41) and association cortex (Wernicke's area) is key. Remember the location of these areas within the temporal lobe and their specific functions, as well as their vascular supply from the MCA superior division. Key differentials include Broca's area (frontal lobe, speech production) and primary motor/visual cortices (frontal/occipital lobes, motor/visual function).

<a id='question-050'></a>

### Question 050: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 1 Focus 50 (rostrally;)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Identify the specific cortical area (Part 1 Focus 50, rostral) and its associated functions, particularly related to motor control and sensory integration, and relate deficits to lesions in this region. (识别大脑皮层特定区域（Part 1 Focus 50， rostral）及其相关功能，特别是与运动控制和感觉整合有关，并将缺陷与该区域的病变联系起来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old right-handed male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with acute onset of right-sided weakness and numbness, predominantly affecting his face and arm. He reports difficulty forming words and understanding simple commands. On examination, his vital signs are stable. Neurological examination reveals right hemiparesis (face > arm > leg), right hemisensory loss (face > arm > leg), and expressive aphasia (difficulty speaking). Cranial nerve examination shows right lower facial weakness (sparing the forehead). Reflexes are brisk on the right side. An emergent non-contrast head CT scan reveals a left middle cerebral artery (MCA) territory infarct.

Which cortical area is most likely affected by this infarct, leading to the patient's specific constellation of symptoms?

(A) Primary Motor Cortex (Brodmann area 4)
(B) Primary Somatosensory Cortex (Brodmann area 3, 1, 2)
(C) Premotor Cortex (Brodmann area 6)
(D) Broca's Area (Brodmann area 44/45)
(E) Wernicke's Area (Brodmann area 22)

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia). This increases the likelihood of a stroke.
- **Acute Onset:** Suggests a vascular event (stroke).
- **Right-sided Weakness & Numbness (Face > Arm > Leg):** Indicates a lesion in the contralateral (left) cerebral hemisphere affecting motor and sensory pathways. The face > arm > leg pattern is characteristic of involvement of the lateral convexity of the cerebral cortex, supplied by the MCA.
- **Difficulty Forming Words (Expressive Aphasia):** Points to damage in Broca's area, responsible for speech production.
- **Difficulty Understanding Commands:** Suggests possible involvement of Wernicke's area or its connections, responsible for language comprehension. However, the primary deficit described is expressive aphasia.
- **Right Lower Facial Weakness (Sparing Forehead):** This pattern indicates an upper motor neuron lesion affecting the contralateral (left) corticobulbar tract, specifically the fibers supplying the lower face. The forehead receives bilateral cortical innervation, hence sparing.
- **CT Scan:** Confirms a left MCA territory infarct. The MCA supplies the lateral surface of the frontal, temporal, and parietal lobes, including the motor cortex, sensory cortex, Broca's area, and Wernicke's area.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the specific cortical area responsible for the *constellation* of symptoms, particularly the expressive aphasia combined with the motor/sensory deficits in the MCA distribution.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Part 1 Focus 50 (Rostral):** This refers to the rostral (anterior) part of the cerebral cortex, specifically the frontal lobe. Key areas in this region include the primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus, Brodmann area 4), premotor cortex (frontal gyrus superior to precentral gyrus, Brodmann area 6), prefrontal cortex, and Broca's area (inferior frontal gyrus, Brodmann areas 44 and 45).
- **MCA Territory:** The left MCA supplies Broca's area (Brodmann areas 44/45), the primary motor cortex (Brodmann area 4) for the face and arm, the primary somatosensory cortex (Brodmann areas 3, 1, 2) for the face and arm, and parts of Wernicke's area (Brodmann area 22) in the temporal lobe.
- **Broca's Area (Brodmann 44/45):** Located in the inferior frontal gyrus of the dominant hemisphere (usually left). It is crucial for speech production (motor aspects of language). Damage leads to expressive aphasia (Broca's aphasia), characterized by difficulty forming words, telegraphic speech, and relatively preserved comprehension.
- **Primary Motor Cortex (Brodmann 4):** Located on the precentral gyrus. Controls voluntary movements. Damage causes contralateral weakness (hemiparesis). The homunculus representation shows the face and hand having large cortical areas, explaining the face > arm > leg pattern of weakness in MCA strokes.
- **Primary Somatosensory Cortex (Brodmann 3, 1, 2):** Located on the postcentral gyrus. Processes sensory information. Damage causes contralateral sensory loss. Similar to the motor cortex, the face and hand have large representations.
- **Premotor Cortex (Brodmann 6):** Located anterior to the primary motor cortex. Involved in planning and sequencing movements. Damage can cause apraxia (difficulty performing learned movements) or subtle motor deficits.
- **Wernicke's Area (Brodmann 22):** Located in the posterior superior temporal gyrus of the dominant hemisphere. Crucial for language comprehension. Damage leads to receptive aphasia (Wernicke's aphasia), characterized by fluent but nonsensical speech and impaired comprehension.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's symptoms (right hemiparesis/hemisensory loss, face > arm > leg, expressive aphasia, sparing of forehead in facial weakness) are classic for a left MCA stroke involving both the motor/sensory cortex and Broca's area. Broca's area is located within the rostral (anterior) part of the cortex (Part 1 Focus 50) supplied by the MCA. The expressive aphasia is the key localizing symptom pointing specifically to Broca's area.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Primary Motor Cortex (Brodmann area 4):** While damage to the primary motor cortex would explain the right hemiparesis (face > arm > leg), it would *not* explain the expressive aphasia. The motor cortex is primarily involved in movement execution, not language production.
- (B) **Primary Somatosensory Cortex (Brodmann area 3, 1, 2):** Damage to the primary somatosensory cortex would explain the right hemisensory loss (face > arm > leg), but not the expressive aphasia or the motor weakness (although sensory deficits often accompany motor deficits in MCA strokes).
- (C) **Premotor Cortex (Brodmann area 6):** Damage to the premotor cortex could cause motor deficits, potentially including apraxia, but it is less likely to cause the prominent expressive aphasia seen in this patient. Broca's area is more directly responsible for speech production.
- (E) **Wernicke's Area (Brodmann area 22):** Damage to Wernicke's area would cause receptive aphasia (difficulty understanding language), not expressive aphasia (difficulty producing language). While Wernicke's area can be affected in MCA strokes, the primary language deficit described here points to Broca's area.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Broca's Area (Brodmann 44/45) | Primary Motor Cortex (Brodmann 4) | Primary Somatosensory Cortex (Brodmann 3,1,2) | Premotor Cortex (Brodmann 6) | Wernicke's Area (Brodmann 22) |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------|---------------------------------|---------------------------------|
| **Location**         | Inferior Frontal Gyrus          | Precentral Gyrus                  | Postcentral Gyrus                            | Superior Frontal Gyrus         | Posterior Superior Temporal Gyrus |
| **Primary Function** | Speech Production (Motor)       | Voluntary Movement Execution       | Sensory Perception                          | Motor Planning/Sequencing      | Language Comprehension          |
| **Lesion Deficit**   | Expressive Aphasia              | Contralateral Hemiparesis         | Contralateral Hemisensory Loss               | Apraxia, Subtle Motor Deficits | Receptive Aphasia              |
| **Part 1 Focus 50?** | Yes (Rostral Frontal Lobe)      | Yes (Rostral Frontal Lobe)         | No (Parietal Lobe)                           | Yes (Rostral Frontal Lobe)      | No (Temporal Lobe)              |
| **MCA Supply?**      | Yes                             | Yes                                | Yes                                          | Yes                             | Yes                             |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The combination of contralateral motor/sensory deficits (face > arm > leg) and expressive aphasia in a patient with an MCA stroke strongly localizes the lesion to Broca's area (Brodmann areas 44/45) within the rostral (anterior) part of the cerebral cortex (Part 1 Focus 50). Differentiating Broca's (expressive) aphasia from Wernicke's (receptive) aphasia is crucial. Remember the location of these areas within the MCA territory and their specific functions.
